<!-- PAGE 3 -->

Digital Financial Platform Engagement and Financial Inclusion 
in the Philippines: Insights on AI Deployment and Policy 
Implications

Nikka C. Pesa  
Mary Grace R. Agner  
Rutcher M. Lacaza

PHILIPPINE INSTITUTE FOR DEVELOPMENT STUDIES

February  2026

---

<!-- PAGE 4 -->

Abstract

This paper examines how digital financial platform engagement influences financial inclusion 
in the Philippines and explores how financial institutions are deploying artificial intelligence 
(AI) in enhancing these platforms. It combines nationally representative data from the World 
Bank  Global  Findex  (2021),  institutional  indicators  from  the  International  Monetary  Fund 
(IMF)  Financial  Access  Survey  (2016–2024),  and  qualitative  insights  from  financial 
institutions and policy experts. From the demand-side a Digital Financial Engagement Index 
was constructed based on individuals’ usage of mobile payments, online banking, and digital 
financial services. Results reveal that digital financial engagement is a strong and consistent 
determinant of financial inclusion: individuals who actively use digital financial platforms are 
significantly more likely to own and use formal financial accounts. However, several barriers 
persist. The lack of money, high perceived costs, documentation issues, and low trust remain 
key constraints among Filipino adults, particularly among low-income groups. From the supply 
side, institutional data from the IMF Financial Access Survey (2016–2024) and insights from 
Key Informant Interviews (KIIs) reveal that financial infrastructure has expanded moderately. 
While AI adoption remains nascent and largely concentrated among large, digitally advanced 
financial institutions, AI technologies such as fraud detection, credit scoring, and chatbots are 
increasingly embedded in the digital platforms where consumer transact. In contrast, smaller 
cooperatives and savings and loan associations continue to face structural and resource-related 
barriers  to  adoption.  The  convergence  of  demand-  and  supply-side  evidence  highlights  that 
digital financial  engagement  serves  both as  a driver and an indicator of  financial  inclusion, 
while AI adoption within these platforms has potential to  further enhance access, security, and 
user  experience.  Overall,  this  study  provides  new  empirical  evidence  that  digital  financial 
engagement  significantly  enhances  financial  inclusion  in  the  Philippines.  The  study  also 
documents  how  financial  institutions  are  strategically  deploying  AI  within  these  digital 
platforms, providing insights for responsible AI adoption that can support inclusive financial 
services. By strengthening digital infrastructure, promoting financial and digital literacy, and 
ensuring responsible AI adoption, the Philippines can transform digital financial platforms into 
a true catalyst for inclusive and sustainable growth.

Keywords:  digital  financial  engagement,  artificial  intelligence,  financial  inclusion,  digital 
platforms, Philippines

Digital Financial Platform Engagement and Financial Inclusion in the 
Philippines: Insights on AI Deployment and Policy Implications

Nikka C. Pesa1, Mary Grace R. Agner2, Rutcher M. Lacaza3

1. Introduction

1.1 Background of the Study

Access to formal financial accounts in the Philippines has developed over the years. With the 
rise  of  the  internet  and  the  emergence  of  electronic  banking  in  the  1980’s,  Filipinos  have 
acquired  formal  financial  accounts  through  automated  teller  machines  (ATMs),  internet 
banking, and digital wallets (Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas n.d.). A basic measure of financial 
inclusion  is  having  possession  of  an  account  within  a  recognized  institution,  which 
encompasses  banks,  e-money  issuers,  and  microfinance  institutions  (Bangko  Sentral  ng 
Pilipinas 2021). Years  of economic growth  have paved  the  way for  several underprivileged 
Filipinos to become part of the middle class which led them to experience the benefits of its 
increased purchasing power (De Gantès et al. 2023). Also, they note that a young techy-savvy 
consumer base is fuelling a heightened demand for innovative financial accounts and services. 
According to the recent  2021 Financial Inclusion Survey, formal account ownership among 
Filipinos has drastically improved, reaching 56% in 2021 compared to 29% in 2019 with e-
money  accounts  now  being  the  most  owned.  However,  when  comparing  it  to  selected 
economies in the Association of Southeast Asian region such as Singapore (97.55%), Thailand 
(95.58%), Malaysia (88.37%), and Viet Nam (56.27%), the World Bank (2021) reports that the 
Philippines has relatively higher percentage of unbanked and unserved population (51.37%). 
Leveraging digital technology can narrow the gap of this segment in accessing and availing 
financial services, purchasing products and services, as well as engaging in other transactions.

As the Philippine financial system continues to adapt with emerging technology, traditional 
universal and commercial banks are now going heavy in offering online/digital banking for 
their  consumers  following  BSP's  Digital  Payments  Transformation  Roadmap  2020-2023. 
Under  this  roadmap,  one  of  the  BSP’s  targets  is  to  have  50%  of  the  total  retail  transaction 
volume be digital. As seen in Figure 1, the BSP reported that 57.4% of the total volume of 
retail payments (5,756.5 billion) in 2024 came from digital payments (3,307 billion), with non-
digital  payments  now  holding  lesser  of  the  share  by  2,449.5  billion  (Bangko  Sentral  ng 
Pilipinas  n.d.).  Targets  for  digital  payments  have  been  increased  to  52  to  54%  under  the 
Philippine Development Plan of 2023 to 2028. Figure 1 also shows that these targets have been 
met since digital retail payments accounted for 57.4% of total transaction volume according to 
the  2024  e-payments  measurement  conducted  by  the  BSP.  Digital  payments  accounted  for 
3.307  billion  while  non-digital  payments  contributed  2.449  billion  to  the  total  transactions 
volume.

Moreover, the 2021 Financial Inclusion Survey reported cash as the leading mode of payment, 
as seen in Figure 2. While this is the case, with the increasing number of Filipinos with bank 
accounts and emergence of digital banks, digital retail payments also increased to 42% in 2022 
(Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas, n.d.). The BSP targeted 50% for 2023 given its recent issuance 
of licenses to 258 digital payment providers. Because of the increase in digital transactions, 
banking  fraud  and  cybercrime  has  also  increased  (De  Vera  2022).  This  is  the  reason  why 
Filipinos are hesitant to put money in banks (Lim 2024), reluctant to use credit products (Jocson 
2023; Yu 2023), and not privy to engaging in more digital transactions (De Gantès et al. 2023).

Artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) are gaining prominence across sectors, 
especially  in  financial  services  (OECD  2021).  Khsetri  (2021)  highlighted  that  the  fast 
advancements in AI and ML are instigating political, economic, and social transformations in 
developing economies. According to (Khsetri 2021), AI's revolutionary effects have reshaped 
every sector of the economy, including financial services, and it has led to a paradigm shift in 
enhancing  financial  inclusion  within  an  economy.  Moreover,  adoption  of  AI  and  ML  in 
financial sector offers financial deepening, efficiency, transformation of financial services and 
consumers’ interaction with the financial services ecosystem (Khsetri 2021). On the other hand,

2

---

<!-- PAGE 9 -->

one should take into account as well that this could likely lead to undesirable effects, such as, 
data privacy vulnerabilities, and unfair outcomes for certain consumers like women (Rizzi and 
Alvarez 2023), and intensifying the digital divide between advanced and developing economies 
(Boukherouaa et al 2021). With these considerations in mind, and building on the discussion 
of De Gantès et al. (2023) and the OECD (2021), it is worthwhile to explore the AI deployment 
in the Philippine financial sector, including its impact both on those who are already financially 
included and on those Filipinos who have yet to be reached by the banking sector and whose 
confidence in the financial sector must be increased.

While the potential of AI to advance financial inclusion is clear, studying its direct impact faces 
significant methodological challenges. Large-scale household surveys do not yet capture direct 
measures  of  AI  awareness,  usage,  or  literacy  (World  Bank  2021).  Moreover,  since  AI 
technologies are often embedded invisibly within digital financial platforms, consumers may 
use  AI-powered  fraud  detection  or  credit  scoring  without  realizing  it.  To  address  these 
constraints, this study adopts a two-sided approach. On the demand side, the analysis examines 
how engagement with digital financial platforms – the primary channels through which AI-
enabled  services  are  delivered  –  influences  financial  inclusion.  On  the  supply  side,  it 
investigates  how  financial  institutions  deploy  AI  within  these  platforms.  Collectively,  this 
framework  captures  both  the  behavioral  foundations  of  AI-enabled  financial  inclusion, 
reflected in users’ digital engagement, and the institutional realities of AI adoption.

1.2 Rationale of the Study

The Philippines has made substantial progress in advancing financial inclusion, with formal 
account  ownership  rising  significantly  in  recent  years.  Despite  these  gains,  a  considerable 
segment of the population remains either unbanked or underbanked due to barriers such as cost, 
distance, lack of documentation, and low trust in financial institutions. At the same time, the 
proliferation of digital payments and e-money accounts highlights both the potential and the 
complexity  of  ensuring  inclusive  and  secure  participation  in  the  financial  system.  Artificial 
intelligence (AI), increasingly embedded within digital financial platforms, offers new tools to 
address  these  challenges  by  improving  credit  assessment,  fraud  detection,  and  personalized 
financial services. However, its  adoption also  raises concerns about data  privacy, consumer 
protection, and the risk of deepening inequalities if safeguards are not in place. This study is 
therefore  undertaken  to  examine  how  digital  financial  platform  engagement  influences 
financial inclusion and how artificial intelligence can be strategically deployed within these 
platforms to enhance accessibility, safety and security, drawing insights from both consumers’ 
behavior and institutional readiness.

1.3 Statement of the Problem

Recent  developments  in  the  Philippine  financial  sector  have  indicated  that  the  economy  is 
undergoing a digital banking revolution (De Gantès et al. 2023) where technology is seen as a 
vehicle to boost the financial literacy of Filipinos (Lim 2024). Specifically, the emergence of 
AI, including ML, and big data, in the financial sector have opened opportunities for financial 
institutions to enhance their presence and reach to Filipino households across all demographic 
classifications, posed challenges on the safety and security of banking and making financial 
transactions  online,  and  created  implications  for  policymakers  to  craft  frameworks  towards 
regulation,  safety,  and  security  in  the  use  of  technology  to  make  transactions.  Against  this 
backdrop, this study poses the following research question: how does engagement with digital 
financial platforms influence financial inclusion in the Philippines, and how are financial

3

---

<!-- PAGE 10 -->

institutions  deploying  artificial  intelligence  within  these  platforms  aiming  to  enhance 
accessibility, safety and security?

1.4 General and Specific Objectives

In  addressing  the  above  research  question,  the  study’s  general  objective  is  to  examine  how 
digital  financial  platform  engagement  influences  financial  inclusion  among  Filipino 
households, and to inform  policymakers  how AI can be  strategically deployed within these 
platforms to  enhance  access, trust, safety,  and security  in digital financial transactions. The 
following specific objectives are also set.

1.  to  examine  how  digital  financial  platform  engagement  affects  financial  inclusion 
outcomes  from  the  demand  side,  and  to  assess  the  current  state  and  potential  of AI 
adoption within financial institutions from the supply side;

2.  to provide empirical evidence on the relationship between digital financial engagement 
and financial inclusion, and on the opportunities and challenges of AI adoption in the 
Philippine financial sector;

3.  identify  barriers  to  financial  inclusion,  including  safety,  security,  trust,  and  cost 
concerns and assess how AI deployment in digital financial platforms can help address 
these barriers; and

4.  to inform policymakers on strategies for strengthening digital financial infrastructure, 
promoting  digital  and  financial  literacy,  and  ensuring  responsible AI  deployment  to 
advance inclusive and secure financial services in the Philippines.

1.5 Significance of the Study

Employing  advanced  digital  technology  in  the  financial  sector  has  much  opportunity  for 
advancing the Philippines’s growth (Open Gov Asia 2021). For instance, the Bangko Sentral 
ng Pilipinas (2018) highlighted the advantages of digital financial inclusion provides greater 
access  to  formal  financial  services,  economic  empowerment  and  reduced  risks  and  costs 
associated with cash. The said institution emphasized its ability to access untapped markets, 
facilitate asset accumulation, boost economic participation, decrease cash handling costs, and 
mitigate the elevated risks of theft and fraudulent activities linked to the informal economy. 
The Philippines is experiencing one of the fastest rates of population growth globally, and the 
bankable  population  is  expected  to  surge  from  65  million  in  2022  to  85  million  by  2030, 
marking a 30% growth (De Gantès et al. 2023).

Understanding the relationship  between  digital financial platform  engagement and  financial 
inclusion, and how artificial intelligence can enhance these platforms, is therefore critical for 
maximizing  these  opportunities.  This  paper  makes  several  contributions  to  the  growing 
literature  on  digital  finance,  AI,  and  financial  inclusion  in  the  Philippines.  First, 
methodologically, the study demonstrates how to research the role of emerging technologies 
when  direct  survey  measures  are  unavailable.  By  examining  digital  financial  platform 
engagement from nationally representative survey data and triangulating this with institutional 
evidence of AI deployment through key informant interviews and administrative data, the study 
provides a comprehensive picture of how technology is reshaping financial inclusion. This two-
sided approach addresses the challenge of studying AI's effects before survey instruments fully 
capture the phenomenon.

4

---

<!-- PAGE 11 -->

Second,  empirically,  the  study  provides  new  evidence  on  the  determinants  of  financial 
inclusion in the Philippines, with particular attention to the role of digital financial engagement 
and the barriers that persist despite technological advancement. The findings directly inform 
the  BSP’s  efforts  to  achieve  two  of  its  four  key  outcomes:  reducing  disparities  in  financial 
inclusion and empowering more consumers to be financially capable (BSP, n.d.).

Third,  from  a  policy  perspective,  the  study  offers  actionable  insights  on  how  AI  can  be 
strategically deployed within digital financial platforms to enhance accessibility, safety, and 
security. As pointed out  by the OECD (2021), policymakers play a crucial role in fostering 
innovation within the financial sector, simultaneously safeguarding the interests of financial 
consumers and investors, and maintaining fairness, orderliness, and transparency in the markets 
associated with such products and services. This study provides evidence-based guidance for 
policymakers on strengthening digital infrastructure, promoting digital and financial literacy, 
and ensuring responsible AI adoption, all of which are essential for translating digital financial 
engagement into truly inclusive outcomes.

Aside from its relevance to the Philippines, this study, especially its recommended strategies 
to  policy  makers,  may  also  be  helpful  to  other  countries,  especially  those  with  significant 
unbanked and unserved populations. Around 76% of the world’s population have an account 
either  at  a  bank  or  a  financial  institution,  including  mobile  money  service  providers.  This 
percentage  may  suggest  good  progress  to  gauge  financial  inclusion  globally;  however, 
considering  the  regional  or  economy-level  view,  there  exists  a  wide  variation  worldwide. 
Among  the  123  surveyed  economies  of  the  Global  Financial  Inclusion  (Global  Findex) 
Database, “account ownership ranges from just 6% in South Sudan to universal ownership in 
high-income economies such as Canada, Germany, and the United Kingdom” (Demirgüç-Kunt 
et al. 2021, p. 15). Figure 3 below highlights further this discrepancy. Different economies may 
have varied ways of addressing this issue on access to financial services; however, this study 
on the Philippines can present useful insights to other countries, especially those facing similar 
challenges in leveraging digital platforms and AI to advance financial inclusion.

Figure 3. Adults with an account (%), 2021

Source: Demirgüç-Kunt et al. 2022

1.6 Scope and Limitation

Given  the  nature  and  objectives  of  this  study,  the  authors  examine  how  digital  financial 
platform  engagement  influences  financial  inclusion,  and  how  artificial  intelligence  is  being 
deployed  within  these  platforms  by  financial  institutions.  The  study  adopts  an  Explanatory 
Sequential Mixed Method Research approach that addresses several important methodological 
limitations.

On  the  demand  side  the  study  uses  data  from  the  World  Bank's  Global  Financial  Inclusion 
(Global Findex) Database is utilized. This database comprises nationally representative surveys 
conducted among approximately 128,000 adults across 123 economies. The study specifically 
uses the 2021 Global Findex Database for the Philippines. However, a key limitation is that the 
Global Findex, like most large-scale household surveys, does not include direct questions about 
AI  awareness,  knowledge,  literacy,  or  usage  (World  Bank  2021).  Therefore,  the  study 
constructs  a  Digital  Financial  Engagement  Index  based  on  individuals'  usage  of  mobile 
payments, online banking, and digital financial services. This index measures engagement with 
the digital platforms through which AI-enabled services are increasingly delivered, rather than 
direct AI exposure or awareness. This limitation means that the demand-side analysis captures 
the  behavioral  foundation  for  AI-enabled  financial  inclusion  (digital  platform  usage)  rather 
than  AI  interaction  itself.  Moreover,  actual  AI  exposure  varies  across  platforms  and 
institutions.  An  individual  using  GCash  may  encounter  different  AI  features  than  someone 
using a small rural bank's digital service, even though both would score similarly on the Digital 
Financial  Engagement  Index.  The  index  cannot  distinguish  between  high-AI  and  low-AI 
platforms based on available survey data. Future research with purpose-designed surveys can 
address  this  limitation  by  directly  measuring  AI  awareness  and  platform-specific  usage 
patterns.

To complement the demand-side analysis and establish the connection to AI, the supply-side 
perspective is captured using the IMF’s Financial Access Survey (2016–2024), alongside key 
informant interviews with policy experts and private financial institutions were conducted to 
capture the supply-side perspective. Findings from both approaches are triangulated to enrich 
and contextualize the quantitative results. This triangulation allows the study to demonstrate 
how digital financial engagement (measured on the demand side) relates to financial inclusion 
outcomes, while documenting the AI deployment patterns (observed on the supply side) within 
the  digital  platforms  that  users  engage  with.  This  two-sided  approach  addresses  the 
methodological  challenge  of  studying  emerging  technologies  before  comprehensive  survey 
instruments exist, though it acknowledges the limitations of indirect measurement. The study 
does not examine the technical specifications or algorithmic details of specific AI systems (e.g., 
neural network architectures, training datasets, or model performance metrics), as the emphasis 
is  on  behavioral  and  institutional  perspectives  rather  than  computer  science  or  engineering 
dimensions of AI deployment.

The study also covers institutional and regulatory aspects related to AI adoption, particularly 
issues  concerning  regulatory  capacity,  stakeholder  engagement,  legal  frameworks,  and 
consumer education. These areas are examined to understand how they influence the integrity 
and security of financial systems in the context of AI-driven financial inclusion. With these 
scope parameters and limitations in mind, the paper aims to guide Philippine policymakers in 
crafting evidence-based polices and institutional frameworks that strengthen digital financial 
infrastructure, promote digital and financial literacy, and ensure responsible AI deployment to 
expand financial access and inclusion.

6

---

<!-- PAGE 13 -->

2. Review of Related Literature and Research Gap

This literature review examines existing research on digital financial platforms and financial 
inclusion.  The  review  begins  with  theoretical  frameworks  for  technology  adoption  (Section 
2.1),  followed  by  empirical  evidence  on  how  digital  financial  platforms  and  AI  influence 
financial inclusion from both consumer and institutional perspectives (Section 2.2). The review 
then examines digital infrastructure and AI readiness in the Philippine context (Section 2.3), 
specific  applications  of  artificial  intelligence  in  financial  services  (Section  2.4);  variables 
commonly used in financial inclusion studies (Section 2.5), and existing policy frameworks 
(Section 2.6). The presentation of the research gap follows.

2.1 Theoretical Frameworks for Technology Adoption

The Technology Acceptance Model (TAM) proposed by Davis (1989) has been instrumental 
in  explaining  users’  behavioral  intentions  toward  adopting  new  technologies.  It  posits 
individuals who perceive a technology as useful and easy to use are more likely to develop a 
positive attitude toward it. In other words, the perceived usefulness and perceived ease of use 
are  the  key  determinants  influencing  attitudes  and  intentions  to  adopt  a  technology.  In  the 
context of digital finance, TAM has been widely used to analyze customers’ intention to adopt 
mobile banking, e-wallets, and other financial technologies (Rahman et al. 2023).

However, while TAM provides valuable insights, it tends to underrepresent the roles of trust, 
perceived  risk,  and  data  security,  factors  that  are  increasingly  critical  in  the  adoption  of 
technologies associated with financial data and user privacy (Manser Payne et al. 2018). These 
limitations are particularly relevant when studying artificial intelligence (AI) applications in 
finance,  where  concerns  about  data  privacy,  algorithmic  transparency,  and  cybersecurity 
strongly shape adoption behavior.

To address these gaps, combining TAM with Diffusion of Innovations (DOI) theory has been 
widely adopted by researchers (Rogers 1962). Within this context, relative advantage is a key 
factor  defined  as  the  degree  to  which  a  new  technology  is  perceived  by  users  as  provding 
greater benefits than existing alternatives. By highlighting the added value introduced by the 
new technology, relative advantage enhances TAM’s perceived usefulness. In the context of 
AI-enabled financial services, this relative advantage may be reflected in improved transaction 
security, personalized financial advice, and enhanced fraud detection, all of which strengthen 
user confidence and increase the intention to adopt.

2.2 Digital Infrastructure and Institutional Readiness

The Philippines' capacity to expand digital financial services and adopt advanced technologies 
depends fundamentally on its digital infrastructure and institutional readiness. Multiple indices 
and  assessments  provide  insights  into  the  country's  current  position  and  challenges  in  this 
regard. The International Monetary Fund in 2023 constructed an AI Preparedness Index (AIPI) 
based on macro-structural indicators. The indicators cover 174 countries' digital infrastructure; 
human capital and labor market policies; innovation and economic integration; and regulation 
and ethics. As shown in Table 1, the Philippines recorded an overall score of 0.50 which was 
slightly  higher  than  Viet  Nam  (0.48)  but  relatively  lower  than  Indonesia  (0.52),  Thailand 
(0.54), and Singapore (0.80). Moreover, the Philippines recorded the following values: Digital 
Infrastructure (0.10), Innovation and Economic Integration (0.12), Human Capital and Labor 
Market Policies (0.15), and Regulation and Ethics (0.12).

7

---

<!-- PAGE 14 -->

Broader  ASEAN-wide  indicators  provide  additional  context  on  the  country's  digital  and  AI 
landscape. As presented in Tables 1 and 2, the Philippines occupies a middle position across 
multiple  dimensions,  not  only  in  AI  preparedness  but  also  in  digital  readiness,  financial 
inclusion indicators, and digital economy size. What distinguishes the country, however, is the 
contrast  between  its  very  high  consumer  interest  in  digital  technologies  and  comparatively 
weak  institutional  capacity.  While  Filipinos  rank among  the  highest  in  the  region  in  digital 
technology  engagement  search  activity,  the  ASEAN  Digital  Integration  Index  shows  that 
digital infrastructure and innovation capability remain the country's weakest pillars, which are 
precisely the areas needed for financial institutions to design, deploy, and scale technology-
enabled services. This structural "curiosity-capacity gap" helps explain institutional adoption 
of  advanced  technologies  such  as  AI  in  the  financial  sector  remains  gradual  despite  strong 
consumer-driven digital engagement.

On the other hand, the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (2024) reported that the adoption of AI 
within the Philippine financial sector is currently limited to specific, narrow applications. The 
BSP noted that financial institutions in the country have reached different stages of maturity in 
terms of AI adoption and governance. Based on their survey of financial institutions, the most 
common AI application is identifying Patterns and Anomalies, which primarily used in Fraud 
and  Anti-Money  Laundering  (AML)  systems.  The  report  also  indicated  that  most  of  these 
institutions remain conservative in their willingness to take risks associated with AI. There are 
existing Philippine studies examining the adoption of AI in terms of customer satisfaction with 
mobile banking services (Carandang 2023 and Lim and Parrocho 2024), banking operations 
and performance (Jaca et al. 2025), and the perceptions of customers and employees of banking 
service and experience (Bragas et al. 2025). Despite these, existing empirical research on how 
digital  financial  platform  engagement  influences  financial  inclusion  using  nationally 
representative data, and how financial institutions are deploying AI in the Philippines remains 
limited. Table 3 shows the summary of the international and local studies on AI in the financial 
sector.

2.3 Digital Financial Platforms and Financial Inclusion

Digital financial platforms, including mobile banking, e-wallets, online payment systems, and 
digital  credit  services,  have  emerged  as  critical  channels  for  expanding  financial  inclusion. 
These platforms reduce transaction costs, improve accessibility, and enable financial services 
to  reach  populations  previously  excluded  from  the  formal  financial  system.  In  a  systematic 
literature review, Fazal, Ahmed, and Nisar (2023) examined how technological innovations, 
including  artificial  intelligence  techniques  are  enhancing  financial  inclusion  in  developing 
economies. Their study indicated that qualitative studies center on the contributing knowledge 
regarding AI risks  and challenges, while quantitative studies focused on people’s behaviors 
towards AI adoption. For instance, the authors identified a study that recommended facilitating 
conditions to induce change in semi-rural women toward technology adoption (Manrai et al. 
2021).  Additionally,  they  highlighted  that  level  of  awareness,  security,  and  privacy  can 
influence finance through mobile penetrations among people (Al-Okaily et al. 2024). Lastly, 
they revealed that fintech innovations adoption is possible through performance expectancy, 
effort expectancy, social influence and facilitating conditions (Rahim et al. 2023).

From the consumer perspective, recent research has examined factors influencing adoption of 
digital financial services and AI-enabled tools within these platforms. Jackson and Cepa (2025) 
found that while consumers recognize the possibilities AI presents, many remain concerned 
about  AI-generated  misinformation  and  data  security.  Based  on  the  Financial  Health  Pulse 
Survey, most consumers (including Asian, Black, Latinx, and White groups) rely on traditional 
sources  like  banks,  personal  finance  websites,  and  finance  professionals  for  financial 
information,  with  AI  tools  such  as  general  chatbots  and  robo-advisors  remaining  less 
commonly used (Jackson and Cepa 2025). The authors concluded that a lack of awareness is a 
primary obstacle, suggesting that improving familiarity with AI tools is key to increasing their 
adoption.

Several  studies  have  confirmed  the  importance  of  AI  and  technology  knowledge  in  driving 
adoption. Noreen, Shafique, Ahmed, and Ashfarq (2023) found that consumers’ knowledge of 
artificial intelligence technology had a significant and positive relationship with the intention 
of AI adoption in the banking sector. Moreover, a study by Lim, Low, Yeo, and Tan (2025) 
found  that  Generation  Z  individuals  are  willing  to  adopt  AI  in  banking,  indicating  their

12

---

<!-- PAGE 19 -->

preference for using AI-enabled technology for managing financial services over consulting a 
human financial advisor. The authors further suggest that since using banking apps requires 
users to be tech-savvy, awareness of technology is crucial prerequisite for adoption. Lastly, 
Balaratnam,  Palaniappan,  Merchant,  Da  Gama-Rose,  Pandita,  Cherukupalli,  and  Kanwar 
(2025) constructed an AI Inclination Index to measure the consumer preferences of AI adoption 
in  banking  and  finance.  One  measurement  being  captured  by  the  AI  Inclination  Index  is 
Technology Comfort which gauges “consumers’ attitude toward trying the latest technology, 
as well as their comfort with using their smartphone in the purchase journey”. The research 
revealed that AI has the highest uptake in digital payments, where consumers are accustomed 
to  its  background  role  in  improving  security  and  convenience.  Yet,  consumers  show  less 
willingness  to  complete  a  transaction  using  AI  when  the  financial  stakes  are  higher,  with 
Technology Comfort scores lower during the "Buy" phase compared to the "Learn" and "Use" 
phases of engagement.

From the institutional perspective, financial service providers have increasingly deployed AI 
and related technologies within digital platforms to improve service delivery, manage risks, 
and expand reach to underserved populations. Kaur and Dharmadhikari (2023) examined how 
major banks promote financial inclusion through the implementation of artificial intelligence 
(AI)-based products and services. Their study indicated that AI is predominantly utilized in the 
form  of  chatbots,  loan  evaluations,  risk  management,  credit  assessments,  bank  enquiries, 
product applications, payment transfers, ATM-related services, and personalized offers (Kaur 
and Dharmadhikari 2023). Kandpal and Khalaf (2020) stressed that AI usage in banking results 
in cost-effective and efficient solutions that cater financial services to the financially excluded 
population.  They  also  contended  that  models  driven  by  big  data  could  be  employed  for 
psychometric  assessments,  capturing  data  that  aids  in  predicting  loan  repayment  behavior, 
applicants' beliefs, performance, attitudes, and integrity. Mhlanga (2020) revealed that AI has 
a strong influence on digital financial inclusion associated with risk detection, measurement 
and  management,  addressing  the  problem  of  information  asymmetry,  availing  customer 
support and helpdesk through chatbots, and fraud detection and cybersecurity.

2.4 AI Applications in Financial Services

Enhancing financial inclusion through digital tools and technologies and uplifting the welfare 
of households and communities have been employed by most countries (Ozili 2021). Kaur and 
Dharmadhikari  (2023)  stated  that  the  next  level  of  policy  decisions  and  regulations,  after 
promoting  rigorous  financial  inclusion,  involves  integrating  technology  to  provide  banking 
services  to  the  mass  population.  One  of  them  is  the  utilization  of  artificial  intelligence.  As 
digital financial platforms have expanded, financial institutions have increasingly integrated 
artificial intelligence technologies to enhance service delivery, improve risk management, and 
expand  reach  to  underserved  populations.  Artificial  intelligence  is  broadly  defined  as  “the 
science  and  engineering  of  making  intelligent  machines,  especially  intelligent  computer 
programs (McCarthy 2007, p. 2). It is related to a similar task using computers to understand 
human intelligence.” Sheikh et al. (2023) pointed out that there is no standard definition of the 
concept  yet.  De  Zúñiga  et  al.  (2024,  p.  1)  presented  an  AI  scholarly  definition  in 
communication  research  “as  the  tangible  real-world  capability  of  non-human  machines  or 
artificial entities to perform, task solve, communicate, interact, and act logically as it occurs 
with  biological  humans.”  Kothandapani  (2022)  described  it  as  the  ability  of  a  machine  to 
process  information  and  make  decisions  or  forecasts  without  needing  specific,  step-by-step 
programming. Bank for International Settlements (2024, p. 93) defined Artificial Intelligence

13

---

<!-- PAGE 20 -->

as  “a  broad  term,  referring  to  computer  systems  performing  tasks  that  require  human-like 
intelligence”.

Kothandapani (2022) stressed that the enhancement of computation capacity and algorithms, 
and data accessibility have evolved the AI applications in the financial services sector. Thakkar 
(2024)  highlighted  how  AI-driven  fintech  solutions,  including  credit  scoring,  chatbots,  and 
fraud detection systems are promoting financial inclusion within emerging markets. According 
to Yanting and Ali (2023), Artificial Intelligence (AI) has significantly enhanced credit scoring 
and risk assessment through the use of non-traditional data sources, facilitating access to loans 
for  individuals  with  limited  credit  histories  and  low  incomes.  They  also  highlighted  AI-
powered customer identification and verification  systems have improved security measures. 
Also,  artificial  intelligence  empowers  fintech  and  bigtech  companies  to  convert  soft 
information collected through social media or alternative methods into hard information (Beck 
2020). Machine learning, a subset of Artificial Intelligence, can be used for evaluating credit 
scoring and enhancing financial inclusion (Bazarbash, 2019; Óskarsdóttir et al., 2019). How et 
al. (2020) acknowledged the AI complexity and so developed a research paper showcasing a 
no-coding human-centric AI-based approach to demonstrate AI for individuals without a strong 
background in computer science. According to them, this approach has the potential to simulate 
interactions between financial profiles and predict individuals' intentions regarding the offered 
financial products. For Telukdarie and Mungar (2023), artificial intelligence, as part of digital 
technology  implementation,  may  serve  as  an  effective  instrument  to  linking  individuals  in 
developing  markets  to  financial  institutions.  However,  despite  AI’s  enumerated  positive 
effects,  it  has  challenges  and  limitations,  such  as  AI-based  FinTech  projects  are  still  in  the 
prototyping stage even in the developed countries, a shortage of AI talent, and concerns about 
negative impacts on employment and privacy.  Nonetheless, Kshetri (2021) believed that these 
challenges are likely to be conquered with maturity of technology. Tan (2025) acknowledged 
the pivotal force of AI in Financial Technology (FinTech), which is revolutionizing analysis, 
automation,  and  decision-making.  He  stated  that  it  reduces  operational  and  labor  costs  and 
improves  the  speed  and  accuracy  of  financial  transactions  such  as  loan  assessments,  fraud 
detection,  and  investment  management.  However,  he  also  pointed  out  the  challenges  of 
integrating  AI  into  FinTech;  concerns  over  algorithmic  bias,  lack  of  transparency,  and  the 
accountability of how AI systems make decisions remain as significant challenges.

2.5 Variables Used in Financial Inclusion Studies

Existing literature on financial inclusion employs a diverse yet convergent set of variables to 
quantify  and  explain  inclusion  outcomes.  Across  multiple  studies,  financial  inclusion  is 
commonly measured  through account ownership, use  of formal savings and  credit  services, 
engagement with digital financial platforms, and, in some cases, composite indices of financial 
inclusion  or  financial  well-being  developed  using  techniques  such  as  principal  component 
analysis  (Debuque-Gonzales  and  Corpus  2021,  2024;  Mojica  and  Mapa  2017;  Tan  2014). 
Explanatory  variables  frequently  encompass  socio-demographic  characteristics,  which 
includes education, income, gender, age, and urban-rural residence, as well as institutional and 
structural factors such as financial literacy, access to digital infrastructure, and the availability 
of formal financial institutions (e.g. bank branches, ATMs).

Moreover, broader contextual variables have been incorporated in cross-country analyses. For 
example, Anyangwe, Vanroose, and Fanta (2024) integrate cultural dimensions derived from 
Hofstede’s framework, while studies such as those by Debuque-Gonzales and Corpus (2021, 
2024),  and  Nandru,  Chendragiri,  and  Velayutham  (2021)  emphasize  digital  access  and

14

---

<!-- PAGE 21 -->

technological readiness as critical determinants of inclusion. Despite variations in context and 
methodology,  the  empirical  evidence  consistently  indicated  that  education,  income,  and 
financial literacy are the most significant predictors of financial inclusion. Conversely, gender, 
regional,  and  socio-economic  disparities  persist  as  barriers,  particularly  in  developing 
economies. Collectively, these studies underscore that financial inclusion is multidimensional 
driven by both access-related factors (e.g. infrastructure, digital connectivity) and capability-
related  factors  (e.g.  education,  literacy,  and  income).  A  summary  of  all  these  studies  is 
presented in Appendix A.

2.6 Existing Policy Frameworks

The  government’s  commitment  to  leveraging  AI  and  digital  innovation  to  strengthen  the 
financial sector is reflected in several key policy frameworks:

National  Strategy  for  Financial  Inclusion  (NSFI)  2022–2028.  The  NSFI  serves  as  the 
Philippines’ comprehensive roadmap for expanding financial inclusion. Although it does not 
explicitly focus on AI, it highlights the central role of digital technologies in lowering service 
delivery costs, promoting innovation, and reaching underserved populations. One of its core 
objectives is the promotion of inclusive digital finance.

National AI Strategy Roadmap 2.0 (2024). Launched by the Department of Trade and Industry, 
the  updated  AI  roadmap  outlines  the  country’s  vision  to  become  a  regional  hub  for  AI.  It 
identifies priority sectors—including financial services—where AI can enhance productivity, 
improve  operational  efficiency,  and  support  innovation.  The  roadmap  also  emphasizes 
capacity-building and the development of supportive research and regulatory ecosystems.

BSP Digital Payments Transformation Roadmap (2020–2023). This roadmap sets the Bangko 
Sentral ng Pilipinas’ strategic direction for accelerating the adoption of digital payments and 
expanding account ownership. It targets a significant shift toward digital retail payments and 
broader  participation  in  the  formal  financial  system,  laying  essential  groundwork  for  AI-
enabled financial services.

BSP  Project  Sapiens  (2024).  Project  Sapiens  is  BSP’s  thematic  review  of  AI  and  machine 
learning applications in the Philippine financial sector. It identifies common use cases—such 
as e-KYC,  fraud detection,  credit scoring, and  recommender systems—as well  as emerging 
risks  and  varying  levels  of  institutional  readiness.  The  BSP  underscores  the  need  for 
proportionate AI governance frameworks tailored to each institution’s risk profile.

Data Privacy Act of 2012. The Data Privacy Act provides the legal foundation for responsible 
data  processing  in  digital  and  AI-enabled  financial  services.  Its  principles  on  transparency, 
fairness, and data protection are central to ensuring that AI applications,  such as  automated 
credit scoring and fraud management, operate in a safe and ethical manner.

National  Cybersecurity  Plan  2023–2028.  The  National  Cybersecurity  Plan  strengthens  the 
country’s defense against cyber threats and supports a secure environment for digital financial 
transactions.  It  reinforces  the  importance  of  resilient  digital  infrastructure,  which  is  a 
prerequisite for safe AI adoption in the financial sector.

Republic Act 12234 (Konektadong Pinoy Act). This law establishes a national framework to 
expand digital connectivity, aiming to reduce the digital divide and ensure broader access to

15

---

<!-- PAGE 22 -->

online services. Improved connectivity supports both digital financial inclusion and the data 
ecosystems required for AI-driven financial services.

Republic  Act  12254  (E-Governance  Act).  This  law  institutionalizes  the  transition  to 
interoperable  and  efficient  digital  public  services  across  government.  By  promoting 
digitization of records and standardized ICT systems, it expands the availability of digital data 
and enhances the enabling environment for AI innovations, including in financial applications.

DOST–PCIEERD ICT and AI Roadmap (2020). This roadmap outlines national priorities for 
AI and ICT research, focusing on innovation, automation, and data-driven development. While 
broad in scope, it reinforces the country’s long-term commitment to building AI capabilities 
that can also benefit the financial sector.

As  AI  continues  to  develop  due  to  advances  in  technology,  policy  frameworks  are  also 
continuously refined to match the challenges that these innovations present especially in the 
financial sector. Through collaborations with different stakeholders and with the support of the 
government, inadequacies  are  aimed to  be resolved, thus  maximizing the potential  of AI in 
increasing financial inclusion in the Philippines.

2.7 Research Gap

Philippine  studies  on  financial  inclusion,  utilizing  nationwide  data,  have  predominantly 
focused on examining its determinants, such as demographic and social variables (Llanto 2015; 
Llanto and Rosellon 2017; Alinsunurin and Alinsunurin 2015), measuring financial inclusion 
indices (Debuque-Gonzales and Corpus 2021), and examining the effect of financial literacy 
on  financial  inclusion  (Desello  and  Agner  2023).  More  recently,  Debuque-Gonzales  and 
Corpus  (2024)  examined  disparities  in  the  ownership  and  use  of  financial  technology, 
particularly mobile money, across education and income levels, highlighting emerging digital 
divides in financial access. While these studies provide valuable insights on digital financial 
access, what remains less explored is a comprehensive examination of digital financial platform 
engagement, encompassing not only mobile money but also online banking, digital payments, 
and  e-commerce  transactions,  and  how  this  broader  pattern  of  digital  behavior  relates  to 
financial  inclusion  outcomes.  Moreover,  research  on  artificial  intelligence  in  the  Philippine 
financial  sector  has  primarily  examined  AI  from  the  supply  side,  focusing  on  institutional 
adoption and customer perceptions (Carandang 2023; Lim and Parrocho 2024; Jaca et al. 2025; 
Bragas et al. 2025), without connecting these developments to consumer engagement patterns.

This study contributes by taking a two-sided approach that bridges these perspectives. On the 
demand side, a comprehensive Digital Financial Engagement Index is constructed to capture 
multiple dimensions of digital financial behavior, including mobile phone ownership, internet 
access,  mobile  payments,  online  banking,  digital  transactions,  and  e-commerce,  and  the 
relationship between this engagement and financial inclusion outcomes are examined. On the 
supply side, the deployment of AI technologies within digital financial platforms by financial 
institutions is documented using institutional data and key informant interviews. By connecting 
consumer behavior with institutional capacity, the study provides a more complete picture of 
how  digital  financial  platforms,  increasingly  enhanced  by  AI  technologies,  can  advance 
financial inclusion in the Philippines. This integrated approach offers insights for policymakers 
on  strengthening  digital  financial  infrastructure,  promoting  digital  literacy,  and  ensuring 
responsible AI deployment to advance inclusive financial services. This is particularly relevant

16

---

<!-- PAGE 23 -->

as digital transactions become mainstream and financial institutions increasingly integrate AI 
into their operations. Figure 4 shows the literature map emphasizing the research gap.

3. Framework, Research Design and Methodology

3.1 Research Framework

In addressing the problem statement and research objectives, a market approach is employed 
looking into both demand- and supply-side facets of financial inclusion. This probes on what 
the  consumers  prefer  (demand-side)  and  what  financial  institutions  can  feasibly  provide  to 
satisfy the needs of the consumers (supply-side). The conceptual framework is illustrated in 
Figure 5.

From the framework presented in Figure 5, the study employed a qualitative analysis to address 
the  problem  statement  and  research  objectives.  An  explanatory  sequential  mixed  method  is 
employed as seen in Figure 6. The research team used a quantitative method to analyze the 
demand-side, followed by qualitative key informant interviews for the supply-side. Following 
this order, the quantitative method is used first, and the qualitative method is used to explore 
unexpected  results.  Hence,  the  qualitative  findings  not  only  enrich  and  contextualize  the 
quantitative  results  but  also  bridge  any  gaps  between  the  two  perspectives,  ensuring  a 
comprehensive understanding of AI’s role in advancing financial inclusion. Afterwards, these 
results were utilized to make interpretations, conclusions, and recommendations.

3.3 Quantitative Analysis (Demand-Side)

To examine how digital financial platform engagement influences financial inclusion from the 
demand-side perspective, the study made use of the Global Financial Inclusion (Global Findex) 
database  2021  for  the  Philippines.  Given  that  the  Global  Findex  does  not  include  direct 
measures of AI awareness, knowledge, or usage, digital financial platform engagement is used 
as a behavioral proxy for individuals’ potential exposure or interaction to AI-enabled financial 
services.  This  approach  recognizes  that  AI  is  embedded  unevenly  across  digital  financial 
platforms and is typically encountered through the use of mobile payments, online banking, 
and digital transactions rather than through directly observable AI usage. The Global Findex 
provides the world’s most complete dataset on financial inclusion as it provides demand-side 
data  for  global  and  regional  cross-country  analysis,  which  enables  researchers  to  create  a 
thorough and multidimensional picture of individuals’ behaviour regarding saving, borrowing, 
making payments, and managing risks (World Bank 2022). The survey data covers 128,000 
respondents in 123 economies, capturing 91 percent of the global population. The data has been 
published every three years since 2011. It has 1,000 cases with 114 variables. Since the Global 
Findex is cross-sectional with a number of nominal-level variables, the data will be subjected 
to cross tabulations and contingency table analysis.

3.3.1 Construction of the Digital Financial Engagement Index

Measuring  individual-level  interaction  to  artificial  intelligence  (AI)  is  challenging  because 
large household surveys, including the Global Findex, do not capture direct AI use, awareness, 
or  literacy  (World  Bank  2021).  Moreover,  the  extent  of  AI  integration  varies  significantly 
across digital financial platforms, with some extensively utilizing AI for functions such as fraud 
detection and credit scoring, while others rely more on traditional rule-based algorithms. These 
platform-level  differences  are  not  observable  in  survey  data  that  simply  asks  whether 
respondents use digital payments.

Given  these  limitations,  this  study  constructs  a  Digital  Financial  Engagement  Index  that 
measures individuals' usage of  and interaction  with  digital financial  platforms and  services. 
Digital financial platforms constitute the primary delivery channels through which consumers 
encounter  AI-enabled  financial  services,  even  when  users  are  unaware  of  the  underlying 
technology (Manta et al. 2025; Deloitte 2025; McKinsey 2025). The index should therefore be 
interpreted as measuring digital financial behavior and platform engagement, the behavioral 
foundation  that  enables  potential  exposure  to  AI-enhanced  services,  rather  than  direct  AI 
literacy or awareness. The connection to AI is established through the convergence of demand-
side  behavior,  reflected  in  platform  usage,  and  supply-side  deployment,  which  documents 
which platforms actually incorporate AI, as discussed in Section 4.2.2.

Building on this  framework, the index uses proxy indicators  of digital  behavior  to estimate 
environments in which individuals are more likely to encounter AI-enabled financial services. 
Contemporary financial platforms, such as mobile banking applications, electronic wallets, and 
online payment systems, increasingly integrate AI functions in back-end operations, including 
automated  fraud  detection,  credit  scoring  algorithms,  recommender  systems,  and  customer 
service  chatbots  (Manta  et  al.  2025;  Deloitte  2025).  As  a  result,  routine  digital  behaviors 
provide a useful indication of exposure to the digital financial ecosystem within which AI is 
being deployed, even though they do not measure AI interaction directly (McKinsey 2025). 
Accordingly, the measure should be interpreted as capturing the behavioral channels through 
which AI-enabled services are delivered, with the understanding that actual AI exposure varies

19

---

<!-- PAGE 26 -->

depending on which specific platforms individuals use and how extensively those platforms 
have  integrated  AI  technologies.  Future  surveys  that  directly  measure  AI  awareness  and 
platform-specific usage could provide more granular insights into heterogeneity in AI exposure 
(Felten et al. 2021).

The construction of the index involved the following steps. First, eight binary variables were 
selected  as  proxies  for  digital  financial  engagement,  covering  aspects  of  digital  access  and 
financial  behavior:  mobile  phone  ownership,  internet  access,  making  any  digital  payment, 
using a mobile phone to pay for in-store purchases, paying bills online, sending money online 
to a relative or friend, making online purchases, and making digital merchant payments. These 
variables  were  chosen  for  their  potential  to  reflect  an  individual’s  engagement  with 
technologies through  which AI applications  are  commonly delivered  (e.g.,  e-wallets, online 
banking  platforms,  and  mobile  apps).  Before  proceeding  with  dimensionality  reduction, 
internal consistency was assessed using Cronbach’s alpha to determine whether the variables 
collectively capture a coherent construct related to Digital financial engagement. The analysis 
yielded a Cronbach’s alpha of 0.82, based on eight (8) binary items and an average inter-item 
covariance of 0.064, indicating good internal reliability.

Second,  the  selected  variables  were  standardized  to  ensure  comparability.  This  step  is 
necessary  because  the  binary  indicators  vary  in  prevalence  and  may  otherwise  contribute 
unevenly to the underlying dimensions in the analysis. Standardization balances the influence 
of  each  variable  and  improves  the  interpretability  of  the  results.  Third,  Multiple 
Correspondence  Analysis  (MCA)  was  applied  to  the  standardized  dataset  to  extract  latent 
dimensions  of  digital  behavior  and  access.  MCA  is  a  dimensionality  reduction  technique 
appropriate  for  categorical  data,  commonly  used  to  derive  composite  indices  from  sets  of 
binary  variables.  The  first  principal  dimension,  which  captures  the  greatest  proportion  of 
explained variance, was retained and used as the basis for constructing the Digital Financial 
Engagement  Index.  Finally,  the index values  were normalized  to  a 0–1 scale,  where higher 
scores indicate greater engagement with digital financial platforms and services.

3.3.2 Regression Analysis

The  regression  models  examine  the  relationship  between  the  Digital  Financial  Engagement 
Index and two key dimensions of financial inclusion, which serve as the dependent variables, 
following  the  framework  of  (Debuque-Gonzales  and  Corpus  2021).  The  first  dimension, 
account ownership, includes indicators such as having a formal financial account, owning a 
debit card associated with a financial account, having a mobile money account, and holding a 
credit card. The second dimension, account usage, refers to behaviours such as formal saving, 
formal borrowing, transactions on remittances through a financial institution, transacting via a 
mobile phone, and online financial transactions using the internet.

Each  of  these  dependent  variables  is  binary  (1  =  yes,  0  =  no)  and  modeled  using  logistic 
regression. The general form of the model for account ownership and usage is:

Before  running  the  regression  models,  descriptive  statistics  were  generated  to  explore  key 
patterns in financial inclusion and barriers to account ownership across socio-economic groups. 
Appendix D shows the average rates of formal financial account ownership, including debit 
and credit card ownership and mobile money use. Appendix E presents the average rates of 
formal  financial  service  usage,  such  as  saving,  borrowing,  and  making  digital  transactions. 
Appendix F summarizes the  most commonly  reported  barriers to account ownership. These 
descriptive  tables  help  highlight  existing  disparities  and  provide  important  context  for 
interpreting the regression results.

3.4 Qualitative Analysis (Supply-Side)

To  provide  a  broader  understanding  of  the  supply-side  enablers  and  constraints  related  to 
financial inclusion, this study utilizes time-series data from the IMF Financial Access Survey 
(FAS)  for  the  Philippines  covering  the  period  2016  to  2024.  The  FAS  provides  detailed 
indicators on the country’s financial access infrastructure and institutional capacity, including 
trends in access points, deposit and credit services, and regulatory frameworks. These data help 
contextualize the evolution of the country’s financial ecosystem and support an assessment of 
its readiness to integrate AI-enhanced financial services.

To complement this institutional data, the study also employs key informant interviews (KIIs) 
with selected policymakers and industry experts. KIIs involve a select group of stakeholders 
and individuals that are chosen given the experience, information, and insights they have on 
the subject at hand (Kumar 1989). Key informants are deemed expert sources of information 
(Marshall, 1996) and can  expand our knowledge  and understanding,  including  nuances  and 
peculiarities, about a specific subject-matter (Cossham and Johanson 2019). The selected key 
informants include experts from organizations involved in financial inclusion such as academe, 
public policy, financial institutions (e.g. banks, cooperatives, associations), and data science.

3.5 Validation

The study employed a triangulation method as a technique to cross verify data from multiple 
sources (Johnson et al. 2017). For the purposes of this study, the data from the demand-side 
analysis (Global Findex survey), supply-side analysis (IMF Financial Access Survey and key 
informant  interviews),  and  literature  review  are  synthesized  to  provide  a  comprehensive 
assessment of financial inclusion and AI deployment in the Philippine financial sector.  The 
triangulation  process  involves  comparing  patterns  observed  in  consumer  behavior  (demand 
side) with institutional capacity and AI adoption patterns (supply side). Quantitative findings 
on digital financial engagement and financial inclusion are validated against qualitative insights 
from  key  informant  interviews  with  financial  sector  experts.  Findings  are  also  assessed  for 
consistency with established literature on technology adoption and financial inclusion.

4. Results of the Study

This section presents a comprehensive overview of financial inclusion in the Philippines by 
examining  both  demand-side  and  supply-side  indicators  related  to  access,  usage,  and 
infrastructure. Drawing  from the 2021  World Bank  Global  Findex  and the 2016–2024  IMF 
Financial  Access  Survey  (FAS),  the  analysis  highlights  key  behavioral  patterns  and

22

---

<!-- PAGE 29 -->

institutional  trends,  identifying  gaps  and  opportunities  for  promoting  broader  and  more 
inclusive financial participation.

4.1 Demand-Side Analysis (World Bank Global Findex 2021)

4.1.1 Account Ownership and Usage Patterns

Table 5 presents key indicators of account ownership and usage among Filipino adults based 
on  World  Bank  Findex  2021  data.  Slightly  more  than  half  of  respondents  (56%)  reported 
owning  a  formal  financial  account,  while  39%  had  a  debit  card  and  24%  had  a  credit  card 
(among those with accounts). Ownership of mobile money accounts was comparatively lower 
at 30%, suggesting that adoption of mobile-based financial services remains in its early stages.

In  terms  of  account  usage,  only  28%  reported  saving  and  16%  reported  borrowing  through 
formal institutions. Among cardholders, 49% used their debit cards and 77% use their credit 
cards, indicating higher engagement among credit users. In addition, 27% of adults reported 
using formal remittance channels, while 39% engaged in mobile-based financial transactions 
and  56%  made  transactions  online.  These  figures  point  to  uneven  patterns  of  financial 
engagement, where digital channels show potential for broader inclusion, even as the use of 
formal credit and savings services remains limited.

4.1.2 Barriers to Financial Inclusion

Table 6 presents the main reasons cited by Filipino adults for not owning a formal financial 
account.  The  most  commonly  reported  barrier  is  lack  of  money  (76%),  suggesting  that 
affordability remains a critical issue in financial inclusion. This is followed by perceptions of 
high  cost  (55%)  and  physical  distance  from  financial  institutions  (40%),  highlighting  both 
economic  and  geographic  constraints.  Documentation  issues  (39%)  and  a  perceived  lack  of 
need for financial services (37%) also emerged as significant impediments. Trust in financial 
institutions is relatively low, with 20% citing it as a reason, while religious concerns (13%) 
were the least cited. Notably, 34% said they did not need an account because a family member 
already had one, suggesting shared use within households. These findings emphasize the need

Table  7  shows  that  access  to  formal  financial  services  remains  limited  for  many  Filipinos.  Only  15.8%  of 
respondents reported borrowing from a financial institution, and 28.2% reported saving using an account at 
a formal institution. However, the overall rates of borrowing and saving were significantly higher, at 61.2% 
and 64.5%, respectively. This gap suggests that a large share of financial activity takes place outside the formal 
sector.

As shown in Table 8, informal borrowing remains widespread: 42.8% of respondents borrowed 
from family or friends, and among a smaller group of 95 respondents, 48.4% borrowed from 
an informal savings club. In addition, 9.5% of respondents reported saving through informal 
savings clubs. These figures  indicate  that  informal  financial mechanisms continue to  play a 
central role in the financial lives of many Filipinos. This reliance may reflect several persistent 
barriers to formal access, such as cost, documentation requirements, or lack of trust (Debuque-
Gonzales and Corpus 2021). Informal practices are especially important in rural or low-income 
settings, where formal infrastructure is often limited. As such, strategies to improve financial 
inclusion should not only expand formal services but also recognize and build upon existing 
informal practices that remain relevant and trusted by underserved communities.

Table 9 presents summary statistics for the variables used to construct the Digital Financial 
Engagement  Index  in  the  Philippines,  covering  indicators  of  digital  access  (such  as  mobile 
phone ownership and internet use) and digital financial behavior (such as online payments and 
money transfers). The data show high levels of mobile phone ownership (96.3%) and internet 
access  (87.4%),  indicating  strong  digital  connectivity.  However,  the  use  of  digital  financial 
services remains moderate. Only 54.7% of respondents made any digital payment, with lower 
uptake  for  specific  activities  such  as  online  bill  payments  (30.2%)  and  digital  merchant 
payments (26.3%). This suggests that while digital access is widespread, active engagement 
with digital finance is still evolving.

4.1.3 Construction of the Digital Financial Engagement Index

To  construct  a  Digital  Financial  Engagement  Index,  a  Multiple  Correspondence  Analysis 
(MCA) was performed on seven categorical variables capturing ownership, access, and usage 
of digital and financial technologies namely, mobile phone ownership, internet access, use of 
any digital payment method, and engagement in specific financial transactions (bill payments, 
remittances, merchant payments, etc.).

Two dimensions were extracted, explaining 94.1% of the total inertia (Dim 1 = 93.9%, Dim 2 
= 0.17%). Since the first dimension accounted for nearly all the variance, it was retained as the 
basis for the Digital Financial Engagement Index. Dimension 1 reflects a gradient of digital 
financial engagement, ranging from individuals with limited access to enabling technologies 
(no mobile phone, no internet, no digital payments) to those who frequently conduct financial 
transactions  using  digital  platforms.  The  largest  positive  contributors  were  participation  in 
online  bill  payment  (fin14_1  =  yes,  12.3%),  remittances  (fin14a  =  yes,  12.9%),  merchant 
payments (merchantpay_dig = yes, 14.4%), and other financial activities (fin14a1 = yes, 9.6%; 
fin14b  =  yes,  7.5%).  Negative  contributions  corresponded  to  lack  of  access  and  non-use  of 
digital  tools  (internetaccess  =  no,  mobileowner  =  no,  anydigpayment  =  no).  The  second 
dimension captured only 0.17% of inertia and exhibited no clear thematic clustering, hence it 
was not used in index construction.

The row scores for the first dimension were normalized to  a 0–1 scale to create the Digital 
Financial  Engagement  Index,  where  higher  values  indicate  greater  engagement  with  digital 
financial technologies. The index ranged from 0 to 1 (mean = 0.44, SD = 0.30), suggesting 
moderate heterogeneity  in digital  financial engagement across respondents. This  index  later 
served  as  a  key  explanatory  variable  in  the  empirical  analysis  linking  Digital  Financial 
Engagement to financial inclusion outcomes. Detailed results of the Multiple Correspondence

25

---

<!-- PAGE 32 -->

Analysis (MCA), including category coordinates and contributions to the retained dimension, 
are presented in Appendix B. Summary statistics of the Digital Financial Engagement Index 
and its key indicators, disaggregated by socio-economic group, are provided in Appendix C.

4.1.4 Regression Analyses

4.1.4.1 Determinants of Account Ownership

Table 10 presents the marginal effects from the logit regressions on various forms of account 
ownership: traditional financial accounts, debit cards, credit cards, and mobile money accounts. 
The estimations use survey weights and standard errors are reported in parentheses.

As hypothesized, the Digital Financial Engagement Index is a strong and consistent predictor 
of account ownership across all types. A one-unit increase in Digital Financial Engagement 
Index is associated with a 78.5 percentage point increase in the likelihood of owning a formal 
account, 48.3 points for debit card ownership, 41.0 points for credit card ownership, and 59.3 
points for mobile money ownership – all statistically significant at the 1% level. This highlights 
the enabling role of digital financial platform engagement in financial access.

In terms of age, older adults (35–44) are significantly more likely to own formal and debit card 
accounts, while seniors (60+) are especially more likely to own credit cards (by 29.3 percentage 
points). Interestingly, mobile money ownership does not significantly differ across age groups, 
and seniors are slightly less likely to use it.

The  effect  of  gender  is  not  statistically  significant  for  any  ownership  type,  suggesting  a 
diminishing gender gap in financial access.

Education  plays  a  more  prominent  role  in  traditional  account  ownership.  Individuals  with 
tertiary education are 16.5 percentage points more  likely to own  a formal  account and  18.4 
points  more  likely  to  own  a  debit  card  compared  to  those  with  only  primary  education. 
However, education does not significantly affect mobile money or credit card ownership in this 
sample.

Across income quintiles, those in the 5th (richest) quintile are more likely to own debit cards 
(13.4 points) and credit cards (22.5 points) than the poorest quintile. However, no strong pattern 
emerges for  mobile money ownership, reinforcing the idea  that such services may be more 
accessible to lower-income groups, or that usage is still limited regardless of income.

Finally, employment status shows that those in the labor force are more likely to own credit 
cards (10.2 percentage points) compared to those out of the workforce. The effect is modest or 
insignificant for other account types.

Table 11 presents the marginal effects from logit regressions examining the likelihood of using 
accounts for various financial purposes, including formal saving, borrowing, debit and credit 
card usage, remittance transactions, and fintech-based usage via mobile and internet platforms. 
Each  column  corresponds  to  a  distinct  financial  behavior,  and  the  marginal  effects  reflect 
percentage point changes in the predicted probability of account usage, holding other factors 
constant.

Digital  financial  engagement  emerges  as  a  consistently  strong  and  statistically  significant 
predictor  across  all forms of account usage.  For  example,  a  one-unit  increase in the  Digital

27

---

<!-- PAGE 34 -->

Financial Engagement Index is associated  with an increase of 34.5 percentage points in the 
likelihood of formal saving (Column 1), 24.4 points for formal borrowing (Column 2), and as 
high as 157.0 percentage points for online usage (Column 7), suggesting that digital financial 
engagement is a key enabler of financial inclusion via both traditional and digital channels.

The relationship between age group  and account usage is generally nonlinear. Compared to 
youth (ages 15–24), older adults (ages 45–59) are significantly more likely to borrow formally 
(8.8  percentage  points),  use  credit  cards  (26.4  percentage  points)  and  remit  formally  (10.4 
percentage  points).  However,  they  are  significantly  less  likely  to  use  online  platforms  for 
financial  transactions  (−8.5  points).  A  similar  negative  relationship  with  online  usage  is 
observed across all older age groups (ages 25 and above) when compared to youth aged 15–
24, underscoring a digital divide by age.

Gender  does  not  appear  to  significantly  predict  any  type  of  account  usage  in  this  model, 
suggesting that the gender gap in account usage might be narrowing or is influenced by other 
intersecting factors like income and education.

In terms of educational attainment, individuals with tertiary education are 8.6 percentage points 
more likely to save formally and 35.8 points more likely to use credit cards than those with 
primary or less schooling. Interestingly, education does not significantly influence mobile or 
online  usage  in  this  model,  which  may  indicate  that  mobile  platforms  are  becoming  more 
accessible regardless of formal education.

Income quintiles show a clear pattern, particularly for debit card use, where individuals in the 
2nd income quintile are 54.5 percentage points more likely to use a debit card than those in the 
poorest quintile. Similarly, those in the 3rd and 5th quintiles are significantly more likely to 
save  using  an  account  at  a  financial  institution.  Online  usage  is  lower  among  the  poorest 
quintile, with the 5th quintile being 7.6 points less likely than the richest, a somewhat surprising 
inverse pattern which warrants further exploration.

Employment  status  is  significantly  associated  with  higher  likelihood  of  formal  saving  and 
borrowing. Being in the labor force is associated with a 6.1 and 4.6 percentage point increase 
in  saving  and  borrowing,  respectively,  compared  to  those  outside  the  workforce.  However, 
employment status does not significantly predict usage of fintech channels such as mobile or 
online platforms.

In summary, these results highlight that digital financial engagement is a key determinant of 
financial  account  usage,  particularly  for  fintech-related  services.  Alongside  education  and 
income,  digital  financial  engagement  significantly  influences  individuals’  ability  to  engage 
with formal financial systems. The strong and consistent relationship between digital financial 
engagement and usage outcomes suggest that promoting digital financial platform adoption can 
help expand financial inclusion, especially among older and lower-income populations. These 
findings offer important guidance for supply-side strategies aimed at improving the reach and 
effectiveness of digital financial services.

4.1.4.3 Determinants of Barriers to Financial Inclusion

Table 12 presents the probit regression results examining the individual-level characteristics 
associated  with  perceived  barriers  to  account  ownership.  Each  column  reflects  a  different 
reason cited by the unbanked for not having a formal financial account. These reasons include

29

---

<!-- PAGE 36 -->

issues of access, affordability, documentation, trust, religion, financial constraints, household-
level decisions, and lack of perceived need.

Digital financial engagement, this study’s variable of interest, shows a statistically significant 
negative  association  with  the  probability  of  citing  lack  of  trust  in  financial  institutions  as  a 
barrier (–29.4 percentage points). This implies that individuals with greater digital financial 
engagement tend to have more trust in financial systems. On the other hand, digital financial 
engagement  is  positively  and  significantly  associated  with  the  likelihood  of  stating  that 
someone in the household already has an account (+35.5 percentage points), suggesting that 
those with greater digital  financial engagement may defer  account  ownership due to shared 
household usage rather than lack of access.

Age-related patterns in the results also reflect a digital divide. Compared to youth (ages 15–
24), mid-aged adults (35–44) are significantly less likely to cite lack of documentation (–16.1 
percentage  points)  and  household-level  saturation  (–15.8  percentage  points)  as  reasons  for 
being unbanked. Interestingly, older adults (45–59) are more likely to cite religion as a barrier 
(+13  percentage  points),  indicating  age-specific  cultural  or  personal  attitudes  that  may 
influence exclusion. Seniors (60+) are notably less likely to cite lack of money as a reason (–
27.7  percentage  points),  potentially  reflecting  retirement  incomes  or  reliance  on  household 
members.

Gender  differences  are  also  evident.  While  most  gender-related  coefficients  are  statistically 
insignificant, females are more likely than males to say that someone in the family already has 
an  account  (+13.1  percentage  points),  suggesting  possible  intra-household  financial 
arrangements where women rely on male relatives' accounts.

Education does not strongly predict most barriers, although the most educated are somewhat 
more likely to cite lack of trust (+14.5 percentage points) and a perceived lack of need (+15 
percentage points) than the least educated, possibly indicating a preference for alternative or 
informal financial instruments. Income plays a more significant role. Compared to the poorest 
quintile, individuals in the top quintiles are substantially less likely to cite cost and distance as 
barriers. For example, those in the fourth income quintile are 17.8 percentage points less likely 
to cite distance, and those in the fifth are 40.2 percentage points less likely to cite high costs. 
Interestingly,  higher-income  individuals  are  more  likely  to  cite  household  saturation  (e.g., 
+22.0  percentage  points  for  the  fourth  quintile  and  +18.8  for  the  fifth),  suggesting  account 
sharing may be a rational choice rather than exclusion due to constraints.

Employment status is also a strong predictor. Individuals in the labor force are significantly 
more  likely to mention  documentation (+18.3 percentage points) and  lack  of money  (+15.5 
percentage points) as barriers, reflecting possible formal requirements and liquidity challenges 
tied to employment.

In summary, the findings emphasize that barriers to financial inclusion in the Philippines are 
shaped  by  a  mix  of  structural  (documentation,  cost),  attitudinal  (trust,  religion),  and 
demographic (age, gender, income) factors. Crucially, digital financial engagement emerges as 
a  key  determinant,  reducing  trust-related  concerns  while  influencing  decisions  related  to 
household  financial  arrangements.  These  insights  suggest  that  promoting  digital  financial 
platform  adoption,  coupled  with  policy  interventions  addressing  both  voluntary  and 
involuntary exclusion, can help expand access to financial services.
