A comparison of the results across ownership, usage, and barriers shows a clear pattern in how 
digital financial engagement influences financial inclusion. The effect is strongest for account

31

---

<!-- PAGE 38 -->

ownership, where a one-unit increase in Digital Financial Engagement Index is associated with 
an  increase  of  about  78  percentage  points  in  the  likelihood  of  having  an  account.  The 
corresponding effects on account usage are positive but smaller, such as 34 percentage points 
for formal saving and 24 percentage points for formal borrowing. The analysis of barriers helps 
explain  this  gap.  Individuals  may  be  able  to  open  accounts  because  digital  financial 
engagement makes access easier, but  continued  usage depends on structural and behavioral 
factors such as income constraints, trust in financial institutions, documentation requirements, 
and household financial arrangements. Overall, digital financial engagement facilitates entry 
into  the  financial  system,  but  active  and  sustained  participation  is  shaped  by  the  broader 
socioeconomic environment in which individuals operate.

4.2 Supply-Side Analysis

4.2.1 Institutional Trends (IMF Financial Access Survey, 2016-2024)

To assess the evolving supply-side landscape of financial inclusion in the Philippines, Tables 
11 and 12 present a  comprehensive set of  indicators  drawn  from the  IMF Financial Access 
Survey (FAS), covering the years 2016 to 2024. Table 13 captures the scale and distribution of 
physical  infrastructure,  particularly  automated  teller  machines  (ATMs)  and  bank  branches, 
while Table 14 summarizes the volume of credit and deposit services across various types of 
financial institutions, including commercial banks, cooperatives, other deposit takers (ODTs), 
and microfinance institutions (MFIs).

As shown in Table 13, physical access to financial services improved incrementally over the 
period. The number of ATMs nationwide increased from 19,084 in 2016 to a peak of 23,422 
in 2022, before declining slightly to 23,352 in 2023 and 23,367 in 2024. In relative terms, ATM 
density rose from 26.86 to 28.43 per 100,000 adults and from 64.00 to 78.32 per 1,000 square 
kilometers  between  2016  and  2023.  This  suggests  moderate  improvements  in  accessibility, 
especially  in  terms  of  geographic  coverage.  In  contrast,  the  number  of  commercial  banks 
remained  stable,  fluctuating  between  42  and  46  institutions.  Branch  density  also  saw  only 
marginal growth: from 20.92 to 24.14 per 1,000 square kilometers and from 8.78 to 8.76 per 
100,000 adults. Similar patterns were observed among ODTs, such as rural and thrift banks, 
whose  branch  densities  plateaued  after  2020.  These  figures  indicate  that  while  physical 
infrastructure has expanded slightly, the pace has slowed in recent years.

Table  14  reflects  substantial  growth  in  credit  and  deposit  capacities,  particularly  within  the 
formal banking sector. Between 2016 and 2024, commercial bank loans grew from ₱4.3 trillion 
to  ₱9.6  trillion,  while  deposits  expanded  from  ₱7.4  trillion  to  ₱14.8  trillion.  This  steady 
increase highlights the resilience and deepening of the formal financial system. Credit unions 
and ODTs also experienced growth, although at a slower and more uneven pace. Credit union 
loans rose from ₱5.3 billion to ₱6.8 billion, while deposits increased from ₱9.4 billion to ₱13.4 
billion. ODT loans and deposits likewise expanded, reaching ₱840.3 billion and ₱1.12 trillion, 
respectively, by 2024. Notably, MFIs showed significant momentum. MFI loans grew from 
₱0.87 billion in 2016 to ₱6.3 billion in 2024, while deposits nearly tripled from ₱6.7 billion to 
₱18.9  billion.  These  trends  point  to  the  growing  relevance  of  MFIs  in  promoting  inclusion 
among lower-income and underserved populations.

4.2.2 Key Informant Interviews Insights

4.2.2.1 Profile of Industry and Public Policy Informants

A total of 12 industry and public policy experts were interviewed for this study as summarized 
in Table 15. The questions revolved around the topics of: (a) State of artificial intelligence and 
its  awareness  in  the  Philippines,  (b)  State  of  financial  inclusion  (c)  Profile  of  financial 
consumers and their financial behavior and level  of financial literacy, and (d) Infrastructure 
and  technology  enablers  and  barriers  for  AI.  The  set  of  questions  is  crafted  based  on  the 
findings of quantitative analysis.

The analysis of the interview transcripts identified key themes related to the above-mentioned

4.2.2.2 Current Status of Artificial Intelligence, Data Analytics and Digital Transformation in

the Financial Sector

The  participants  provided  their  perspectives  about  AI  adoption  in  the  financial  sector.  One 
participant  commented  that  “the  financial  sector  has  varying  levels  of  AI  adoption  among 
financial  institutions,  from  advanced  digital  banks  to  smaller  rural  banks  that  are  still 
exploring  AI.”  The  interviewee  shared  that  this  is  rooted  in  the  differences  in  their  focus, 
priorities,  and  strategies.  The  interviewee  further  remarked  that  “AI  adoption  is  still  very 
nascent among institutions.” To offer insights, Dr. Adrienne Heinrich, Head of AI Center of 
Excellence of UnionBank, mentioned that “the organization uses a combination of self-created 
AI solutions and AI solutions provided by partners and vendors.” Another participant from a 
Universal Bank stated that the organization also utilizes AI-generated insights from external 
providers. On the other hand, participants from the Savings and Loan Association expressed 
that although they are aware of Artificial Intelligence applications, its implementation in the 
institution has not yet materialized. They cited two reasons: “their distance from Metro Manila 
means  no  one  has  introduced  it  to  them,  and  they  believe  that  AI  applications  are  not  yet

34

---

<!-- PAGE 41 -->

necessary  for  their  current  market  segment.”  Furthermore,  the  participant  from  the  Credit 
Cooperative  expressed  interest  in  leveraging  AI  and  digital  tools to  improve  efficiency  and 
accessibility; however, “…at the moment, AI is not yet organically needed.” He highlighted 
the organization wants to expand services beyond credit to become multi-purpose, with a new 
grocery initiative aimed at increasing returns and attracting more members.

In terms of data analytics and digital transformation in relation to AI, Dr. Ruel Maningas, the 
Vice  Dean  of  UA&P  School  of  Management  and  Program  Director  of  Master  in  Applied 
Business Analytics (MABA) Program, stated the importance of digital transformation in local 
government and microfinance organizations, citing past projects like the Farmers Information 
Technology Services in the 1990s. He emphasized the need for data governance and quality 
before implementing analytics and AI, citing examples of microfinance institutions in Cebu 
and Manila that have automated systems but limited use of analytics. He also touched on the 
present  challenges  of  managing  large  amounts  of  complex  data  and  the  importance  of 
establishing a single source of truth for business trust.

The  general  consensus  from  participants  highlighted  that  AI  applications  are  utilized  for 
enhancing “Know Your Customer” (KYC and eKYC processes), fraud detection and fighting 
crimes, transaction monitoring, customer experience, credit scoring, risk management, tracking 
policy rates and yields, and virtual customer service assistants. One participant commented that 
“AI is primarily used for internal processes for  work to be more efficient.” Based on these 
findings, the institutions appear to be in different stages of AI maturity.

4.2.2.3 Opportunities and Challenges of Artificial Intelligence and Financial Inclusion

The  participants  also  elaborated  the  potentials  and  concerns  about  expanding  financial 
inclusion through AI. Dr. Heinrich shared her experience of moving to the Philippines 6.5 years 
ago and observing the inefficiencies in financial services compared to Europe. She noted that 
many Filipinos lack bank accounts and that the pandemic accelerated digital transformation in 
the  financial  sector.  She  also  noted  that  one  of  the  consequences  of  the  pandemic  in  the 
Philippines was the high incidence of phishing scams. The National Bureau of Investigation's 
Cybercrime Division reported in August 2020 a 200% increase in phishing campaigns since 
the start of the lockdowns in March 2020. This made phishing the top cybercrime committed 
in the country during that period. She believes that “this challenge stems from the Philippines 
being  in  the  nascent  stages  of  its  digital  transformation  and  cybersecurity  evolution.”  She 
stated  that  “studies  suggest  greater  Digital  Financial  Engagement  leading  to  both  higher 
digital literacy and awareness of potential scams.” Moreover, she highlighted that only 2% of 
Filipinos  can  correctly  answer  basic  financial  literacy  questions  according  to  BSP’s  2021 
Financial Inclusion Survey, emphasizing the need for improved financial and digital education. 
Lastly, she agreed that financial literacy and digital literacy should be developed together to 
help  Filipinos  make  informed  decisions  about  managing  their  money  through  digital  tools. 
These  echo  the  sentiment  that  financial  literacy,  living  within  means,  and  become 
entrepreneurial are key focus areas for education for the Credit Cooperative members.

Mr.  Jared  Martin  Desello  of  Japan  University  of  Economics  who  is  an  expert  in  financial 
inclusion  and  financial  literacy  compared  Japan's  efficient  banking  processes  with  the 
Philippines, where cultural barriers and intimidating bank environments can hinder financial 
inclusion. He observed “that while targeted financial literacy programs for older adults are 
common  Japan,  he  had  not  observed  this  in  the  Philippines.”  He  also  noted  that  digital 
platforms have improved access in the Philippines, but the specific needs of customers should

35

---

<!-- PAGE 42 -->

guide the development of financial products and services. Individuals at different life stages 
would  have  differing  demands  on  what  financial  products  or  services  they  need.  He  also 
mentioned the importance of language settings in financial apps, noting that some customers 
prefer  English  while  others  prefer  Filipino.  For  him,  among  the  characteristics  of  a  good 
financial  inclusion  system  are  ease  of  use  and  access.  He  also  stressed  the  importance  of 
financial  literacy  and  self-responsibility  in  retirement  planning  using  Japan  as  an  example 
where government promotes early investment  through  tax-free incentives  and robust digital 
platforms. Based  on his research  on retirement  savings  in the Philippines, he  noted  that the 
benefits of the current pension system will be insufficient to cover post-retirement expenses 
and  will  rely  heavily  on  individual  savings  or  investment  responsibility.  He  observed  that 
Japan’s government has been proactive by setting a recommended savings target of 20 million 
yen, an amount meant to supplement pension benefits, which encourages individuals plan for 
retirement. Moreover, he explained that private companies in the Philippines are more active 
in promoting financial inclusion, often targeting specific demographics like stock investors. He 
suggested  that  microfinance  institutions  and  cooperatives  could  be  effective  partners  for 
targeting underserved populations.

The  participant  from  a  Universal  Bank  pointed  out  “….the  digital  divide  and  age-related 
differences in financial behavior, with ultra-high-net-worth clients still conservative and risk-
averse in their investment approaches despite more sophisticated knowledge.” Furthermore, 
“these clients are more likely to transfer between banks for slightly higher yields, emphasizing 
the  importance  of  branch  managers  in  maintaining  client  relationships.”  She  observed  that 
younger clients are more open to digital financial services. In terms of financial literacy, she 
explained that while her team focuses on macroeconomic and investment analysis, they have a 
group within the bank dedicated to provide for financial inclusion, including financial literacy 
initiatives. She emphasized that financial awareness and proper understanding of products are 
important even before starting to invest, noting that there is usually a mismatch on the clients’ 
risk appetite and the products that they want to invest in. She also highlighted that gambling is 
affecting  consumer  spending  patterns  and  may  be  affecting  household  savings.  In  terms  of 
financial inclusion and digitization challenges in the country, she explained that while many 
Filipinos  use  digital  wallets  like  GCash  for  transactions,  traditional  banks  offer  more 
comprehensive services and investment opportunities, though high maintenance costs and low 
disposable  income  remain  barriers  to  broader  financial  inclusion.  There  are  two  points  she 
stressed in relation  to these challenges: system  automation could  lead  to  job  losses  in bank 
operations and the importance of clean, high-quality data for measuring financial inclusion.

The  public  policy  expert  reiterated  AI's  role  in  financial  inclusion  and  credit  scoring, 
particularly for digital banks. The interviewee acknowledged that “while AI is a valuable tool, 
it can potentially exclude vulnerable borrowers due to lack of sufficient data to properly predict 
the behavior of these types of borrowers.” The interviewee noted that banks are still learning 
and improving their credit scoring systems, especially for customers from rural or low-income 
groups.  While  AI  has  potential  to  enhance  financial  inclusion,  particularly  through  digital 
banks targeting unbanked populations, there are still challenges in lending to certain segments 
and  refining  credit  scoring  models  for  those  without  traditional  financial  footprints.  Due  to 
these constraints, these people may eventually end up availing services from informal lending 
institutions. The interviewee pointed out how banks within larger ecosystems are able to gather 
more comprehensive customer data and refine their credit scoring approaches over time.

The interviewee also emphasized the need for scam prevention and financial literacy education 
for vulnerable populations. In terms of the challenges on financial inclusion in the Philippines,

36

---

<!-- PAGE 43 -->

particularly  why  people  are  hesitant  to  open  formal  financial  accounts,  the  interviewee 
explained that consumer confidence issues, lack of knowledge about digital banking benefits, 
fraud concerns, and infrastructure limitations, including poor internet connectivity and lack of 
formal  documentation,  are  key  barriers.  The  interviewee  noted  that  the  BSP's  digital 
transformation  roadmap  for  three  years  (i.e.  2020  to  2023)  aims  to  achieve  70%  financial 
account penetration and 50% digital retail transactions, with initiatives like simplified account-
opening processes and e-wallets helping to address these challenges. On the consumer side, the 
interviewee noted that “while some consumers are aware of AI through tools like ChatGPT, 
many others are not aware of AI's presence in financial services, such as automated payment 
reminders.” The interviewee discussed the need for greater consumer education about AI and 
its implications, as well as the development of governance frameworks to guide AI adoption in 
government institutions. The interviewee emphasized the importance of helping stakeholders 
understand and adapt to AI technology to avoid displacement in the workforce.

Participants from the Savings and Loans Association acknowledged that AI already exists, and 
it is a matter of time before they embrace it. However, they still need to upgrade their current 
infrastructure, and they would want to limit it and still have human intervention as this would 
affect their employees. They are open to adopting AI, influenced by its use among their private 
bank  partners.  However,  this  adoption  is  contingent  upon  two  key  conditions:  improved 
internet connectivity and tightened cybersecurity measures to ensure their members' peace of 
mind. The institution digitized its recording, accounting, and membership systems, as well as 
the conduct of elections, several years ago to comply with BSP requirements. It has an existing 
IT infrastructure that can support inter-business operations and cater to its well-defined market. 
However, a significant challenge is internet connectivity. They have observed that the internet 
is much faster in National Capital Region than in their location. In addition, one of the key 
officials raised that “their technology is not as advanced as that of larger financial institutions, 
especially those in Metro Manila.”

Drawing  from  their  perspectives,  they  highlighted  major  points  with  regard  to  artificial 
intelligence and financial inclusion. They underscore the importance of consumer education 
concerning AI, digital literacy, and financial literacy - particularly for the vulnerable sector - 
to  ensure  these  individuals  can  make  informed  decisions  about  managing  their  money. 
Furthermore,  financial  awareness  and  a  proper  understanding  of  products  are  also  crucial 
before investing,  which  helps align the  client’s risk appetite with the products they choose, 
especially for those in the banked segment. The development of financial products and services 
should  be  guided  by  the  specific  needs  of  customers  across  different  life  stages  and  socio-
economic backgrounds.  Moreover,  human relationships remain necessary  to  maintain client 
connections,  and  there  is  a  need  for  development  of  governance  frameworks  to  guide  AI 
adoption in government institutions. On the other hand, one pointed out that there should be 
clean,  high-quality  data  for  measuring  financial  inclusion.  Another  participant  noted  that 
people exposed to AI tend to have higher digital literacy and greater awareness of potential 
scams.

Regarding  challenges,  they  recognized  that  cybercrimes,  the  digital  divide,  age-related 
differences, conservatism and risk-averse behaviors deter individuals from taking advantage of 
banking  services.  The  key  barriers  leading  to  consumer  confidence  issues  are  lack  of 
knowledge  about  digital  banking  benefits,  lack  of  funds,  fraud  concerns,  and  infrastructure 
limitations.  There  is  also  a  risk  of  data  and  algorithms  being  biased  against  unbanked 
populations in digital banking.

37

---

<!-- PAGE 44 -->

4.2.2.4 Current Initiatives on Artificial Intelligence and Financial Inclusion

The  participants  presented  their  institutions  projects  to  promote  financial  inclusion,  and 
leveraging AI.  Dr. Heinrich noted that while many households interact with AI daily through 
various services, UnionBank makes a deliberate effort to ensure their AI-powered features are 
visible and accessible to customers. This includes initiatives showcased on their website and 
the implementation of their Explainable & Responsible AI Toolkit. She highlighted the bank’s 
proactive approach in reducing discrimination in lending decisions by leveraging AI. She also 
emphasized UnionBank’s commitment to engaging customers directly with AI-driven services 
while maintaining a high level of transparency regarding how AI is utilized.

Dr. Heinrich outlined four key components of readiness for digital financial services: digital 
access, financial literacy and trust, active usage, and an enabling environment. She explained 
that UnionBank is addressing these with multiple initiatives, one of these being the Bank on 
Wheels, which provides mobile banking services to rural areas and disaster-affected regions. 
The  bank  is  also  actively  enhancing  customer  support  and  lowering  entry  barriers  for  new 
users.

She also emphasized the importance of a human-centric approach in designing AI features and 
products. She highlighted UnionBank's commitment to responsible AI through an explainable 
and  responsible  AI  toolkit  that  assesses  AI  models  for  bias  and  transparency  before 
deployment. The bank also prioritizes building customer trust by being transparent about their 
AI  practices  and  actively  engaging  with  users  to  address  their  concerns.  Additionally,  she 
discussed  exploring  alternative  data  sources  to  expand  access  to  financial  services  for 
marginalized groups in the Philippines.

Also, UnionBank is dedicated to enhancing financial inclusion in the Philippines, including 
rural and low-income populations. Dr. Heinrich mentioned how UnionBank, through its fintech 
subsidiary UBX, leverages the blockchain-powered i2i platform to deliver essential cash and 
financial services to remote and underserved areas across the Philippines. This initiative has 
significantly  advanced  financial  inclusion  by  connecting  rural  banks  and  community-based 
financial  institutions  to  the  broader  financial  ecosystem.  Additionally,  the  bank  has 
implemented AI in customer service to enhance efficiency. She also touched on the importance 
of  word-of-mouth  referrals  in  building  customer  trust  and  the  bank's  strategy  to  cater  to 
different segments through subsidiaries like City Savings Bank, which targets specific groups 
such  as  public-school  teachers  and  government  workers.  UnionBank’s  subsidiaries  include 
Banko  Kabayan,  which  serves  rural  and  semi-urban  areas,  PetNet  for  remittances  targeting 
OFWs and small businesses, and UnionDigital, a digital bank focused on underserved segments 
without minimum balance requirements.

According to the participant from the other universal bank, the institution aims to increase its 
banking  services  to  reach  100%  of  Filipinos,  particularly  concentrating  on  underserved 
populations.  This  is  possible  through  their  rural  bank  subsidiaries  which  manages  various 
branches and kiosks in provinces or remote areas. The participant stated that “their extensive 
network of branches, which is largest among its competitors, and emphasized the importance 
of physical branches in building trust among Filipinos, especially in remote areas.”

Based on the above-mentioned initiatives, universal banks are more equipped and aggressive 
in  incorporating  artificial  intelligence  in  their  services  and  promoting  financial  inclusion 
especially in remote and underserved areas.

38

---

<!-- PAGE 45 -->

4.2.2.5 The Way Forward: Harnessing Artificial Intelligence for Financial Inclusion

Dr.  Maningas  emphasized  the  importance  of  data  quality  and  analytics  in  decision-making, 
emphasizing  that  actionable  insights  derived  from  high-quality  data  can  support  effective 
interventions.  He  highlighted  the  need  to  validate  analytics  results  and  the  role  of  AI  in 
predictive analytics for identifying at-risk groups. He suggested that one of the most effective 
interventions after identifying such groups is considering factors like cost of production and 
competitor  pricing.  He  also  highlighted  the  drivers  of  AI  and  data  analytics  in  financial 
inclusion, including technology availability, stakeholder recognition, industry guidance, and 
policy  development.  He  emphasized  the  importance  of  data  quality  and  ethics  in  AI 
implementation,  urging  government  and  private  sector  support  for  training  and  capacity 
building. Finally, he shared insights on how data analytics and AI can support, but not replace, 
human  decision-making  in  the  financial  sector.  He  emphasized  the  importance  of  digital 
transformation and digitization in companies and institutions, highlighting the use of AI tools 
for data processing and analysis. He also mentioned advocating for digital transformation at 
the local government level to improve data management and decision-making.

Dr.  Heinrich  explained  that  trust  in  AI  requires  providing  theoretical  knowledge  about  its 
capabilities and potential risks, such as deepfakes and biased decision-making. She emphasized 
the importance of practical experience  with AI  and guidance from tutors or communities to 
build trust. She also highlighted the gender gap in accessing loans in the Philippines and how 
AI  can  contribute  to  fairer  treatment  if  properly  trained.   Driven  by  the  mission  to  broaden 
financial access for all Filipinos, UnionBank is proactively seeking innovative approaches to 
foster greater financial inclusion across the country. She emphasized the need for public-private 
partnerships,  improved  internet  and  electricity  access  in  remote  areas,  and  educational 
programs  to  help  underserved  areas  and  population  groups  like  older  generations  embrace 
digital banking. Dr. Heinrich suggested that the government could play a role in developing 
three-phase  programs  combining  theoretical  learning,  hands-on  experience,  and  community 
guidance to promote digital financial inclusion.

Participants from the Savings and Loans Association wish to cater to reach more clients to avail 
of their services. However, they expressed concerns that fulfilling numerous requirements and 
adhering  to  various  guidelines  and  policies  serves  as  a  barrier  to  business  expansion  and 
increasing their membership nationwide. They hope that “the government will be more flexible 
in  this  regard”.  Additionally,  they  fear  growing  competition  from  large  players  and  other 
Savings and Loan Institutions within their market segment. They are also concerned about the 
potential  influence  of  larger  financial  competitors  in  shaping  these  guidelines  and  policies, 
which they worry may be biased in favor of these major rivals. The participant from the Credit 
Cooperative also pointed out the tedious government requirements.

For  Mr.  Desello,  he  noted  that  he  had  not  seen  significant  use  cases  of  AI  in  educational 
platforms for financial literacy, with most training still relying on human instructors. With all 
these  observations,  he  aspires  that  “AI  can  be  used  as  leverage  to  broaden  the  financial 
education and target the underserved population in the Philippines.” For the participant from 
the  Universal  Bank,  she  stressed  the  challenges  and  importance  of  implementing  industry 
roadmaps  particularly  for  government  initiatives  and  financial  inclusion  projects.  She 
emphasized  “the  need  for  stricter  execution  and  widespread  awareness  to  ensure  effective 
outcomes.”

39

---

<!-- PAGE 46 -->

Finally,  according  to  the  public  policy  expert,  there  is  a  need  for  a  whole-of-government 
approach  to  address  AI  awareness  across  sectors.  He  highlighted  the  importance  of 
coordinating with other government agencies to develop an AI education roadmap, similar to 
existing cybersecurity initiatives.

In summary, the participants shared salient points in leveraging AI toward financial inclusion. 
First, quality of data, technology availability, stakeholder recognition, industry guidance, and 
policy development are the primary drivers of AI and data analytics for enhancing financial 
inclusion.  Second,  providing  practical  experiences  with  AI  and  guidance  from  tutors  can 
increase trust in AI. Third, there is a need for public-private partnerships to improve internet 
and electricity access in remote areas and develop education programs that help underserved 
areas  and  older  population;  this  partnership  should  also  focus  on  developing  programs  for 
enhancing digital financial  inclusion and  knowledge  about AI.  Fourth,  relaxing  government 
requirements is necessary to facilitate expanding the reach of financial institutions.  Fifth, AI 
should also be used for financial education. Lastly, there should be the development of an AI 
education roadmap and stronger enforcement of that roadmap.

5. Discussion and Conclusion

This  study  addresses  a  critical  research  gap  in  the  Philippine  literature  by  examining  how 
digital financial engagement influences financial inclusion from both the demand- and supply-
side  perspectives.  Earlier  studies  have  primarily  concentrated  on  demographic  and  socio-
economic  determinants  of  financial  inclusion  (Llanto  2015;  Llanto  and  Rosellon  2017; 
Debuque-Gonzales and Corpus 2021; Debuque-Gonzales and Corpus 2024) or on measuring 
financial inclusion indices (Debuque-Gonzales and Corpus 2021; Alinsunurin and Alinsunurin 
2015).  Meanwhile,  research  on  AI  has  largely  focused  on  the  supply-side  applications  of 
technology in the financial sector, highlighting the roles of chatbots, credit scoring, and risk 
management  systems  (Kaur  and  Dharmadhikari  2023;  Mhlanga  2020;  Kandpal  and  Khalaf 
2020).  Few  empirical  studies  have  examined  how  AI  awareness,  digital  readiness,  and 
consumer  behavior  interact  to  shape  financial  inclusion  outcomes  in  the  Philippines 
(Carandang 2023; Lim and Parrocho 2024; Jaca et al. 2025; Bragas et al. 2025). This study 
responds  to  this  gap  by  examining  how  digital  financial  platform  engagement  influences 
financial  inclusion  and  how  AI  is  being  deployed  within  these  platforms  to  enhance 
accessibility  and  security  in  digital  transactions.  It  combines  nationally  representative  data 
from the World Bank Global Findex (2021), institutional indicators from the IMF Financial 
Access  Survey  (2016–2024),  and  qualitative  insights  from  financial  institutions  and  policy 
experts,  thereby  providing  empirical  evidence  of  how  digital  financial  engagement  shapes 
financial inclusion outcomes, while documenting AI deployment patterns within the financial 
sector.

5.1 Demand Side Evidence on Digital Financial Engagement

Results from the demand-side analysis reveal that digital financial engagement is a strong and 
consistent determinant of financial inclusion. The constructed Digital Financial Engagement 
Index shows that individuals who actively use digital financial platforms  are significantly more 
likely  to  own  and  use  formal  financial  accounts.  While  digital  financial  engagement  is 
positively  associated  with  a  wide  range  of  financial  activities,  the  magnitude  of  the  effect 
becomes  more  modest  for  more  complex  behaviors  such  as  saving,  borrowing,  and  digital 
transactions.  These  findings  are  consistent  with  international  evidence  (Fazal  et  al.  2023;

40

---

<!-- PAGE 47 -->

Noreen et al. 2023; Lim et al. 2025), which emphasizes that engagement with digital financial 
platforms is a critical enabler of financial participation.

However,  several  barriers  persist.  The  lack  of  money,  high  perceived  costs,  documentation 
issues, and low trust remain key constraints among Filipino adults, particularly among low-
income groups. Interestingly, the regression results among unbanked respondents indicate that 
higher digital financial engagement significantly reduces the likelihood of citing “lack of trust” 
as  a  reason  for  not  owning  an  account.  This  suggests  that  greater  familiarity  with  digital 
financial  platforms  may  help  build  confidence  in  formal  financial  institutions  and  digital 
transactions.  The  results  also  reveal  that  while  the  gender  gap  in  account  ownership  is 
narrowing, there remain  persistent socioeconomic and generational divides, where younger, 
more educated, and higher-income groups exhibited greater financial engagement compared to 
older  or  poorer  populations.  Despite  the  Philippines’  broad  access  to  mobile  and  internet 
networks,  digital  financial  engagement  remains  uneven,  suggesting  that  connectivity  alone 
does not guarantee inclusion. This gap emphasizes the importance of translating digital access 
into effective and trusted financial usage.

Taken together, these results show that digital financial engagement does not affect all stages 
of financial inclusion in the same way. It seems to matter most at the point of entry by helping 
people open accounts and get connected to digital financial services. But once people already 
have  an  account,  its  influence  becomes  weaker.  More  complex  behaviors  such  as  saving 
regularly,  borrowing,  or  using  accounts  for  transactions  still  depend  heavily  on  a  person’s 
income  situation,  confidence  in  the  system,  and  ability  to  meet  requirements.  Thus,  while 
digital  financial  engagement  opens  the  door  to  participation,  the  extent  of  sustained  usage 
remains shaped by the socioeconomic environment in which individuals operate.

5.2 Supply Side Evidence on Financial Infrastructure and AI Deployment

From the supply side, institutional data from the IMF Financial Access Survey (2016–2024) 
and  insights  from  KIIs  reveal  that  financial  infrastructure  has  expanded  moderately,  with 
growth in the number of ATMs, deposits, and loans, particularly within commercial banks and 
microfinance  institutions.  Yet,  AI  adoption  remains  concentrated  among  large,  digitally 
advanced  financial  institutions,  while  smaller  cooperative  and  savings  and  loan  association 
continue to face significant barriers.

KII participants consistently observed that AI adoption in the Philippine financial sector is still 
at  a  nascent  stage,  with  institutions  exhibiting  varying  levels  of  digital  maturity.  Universal 
banks  have  made  significant  progress  in  applying  AI  for  customer  engagement,  risk 
management, and fraud detection, while rural and community-based financial entities continue 
to  face  constraints  stemming  from  poor  internet  connectivity,  cybersecurity  risks,  and 
regulatory burdens. As emphasized by Dr. Adrienne Heinrich (UnionBank), the key challenges 
lie  in  ensuring  data  quality,  algorithmic  fairness,  and  public  trust  in  AI  systems.  Other  key 
informants reiterated  that  the digital  divide, infrastructural gaps, and lack of digital  literacy 
continue to hinder smaller institutions from fully integrating AI into their operations, even as 
they recognize its potential to improve accessibility and operational efficiency.

Amid  these  challenges,  both  industry  and  policy  experts  acknowledged  that  AI  holds 
tremendous potential to enhance inclusion through improved credit scoring, fraud prevention, 
and operational efficiency, provided that strong governance frameworks and responsible AI 
practices  are  in  place.  These  insights  reinforce  the  literature’s  argument  that  AI  can  bridge

41

---

<!-- PAGE 48 -->

information  asymmetries  and  extend  financial  services  to  the  unbanked  (Telukdarie  and 
Mungar  2023;  Bazarbash  2019),  but  only  when  institutions  and  consumers  are  equally 
equipped to engage with technology.

5.3 Convergence of Demand Side and Supply Side Evidence

The  convergence  of  demand-  and  supply-side  evidence  highlights  that  digital  financial 
engagement serves both as a driver and an indicator of financial inclusion. On the demand side, 
greater digital engagement empowers consumers to use financial services more confidently and 
effectively.  On  the  supply  side,  the  readiness  of  financial  institutions  such  as  banks  and 
cooperatives,  reflected  in  their  technological  capabilities,  AI  deployment  strategies,  
governance frameworks, and risk management systems, determines how accessible, affordable, 
and secure these services become. These findings reaffirm the literature’s call for a balanced, 
human-centered  approach  to  digital  financial  transformation  that  combines  technological 
innovation with education, trust-building, and inclusive system design.

Importantly, while our demand-side analysis measures digital financial platform engagement 
rather than direct AI exposure or interaction, the connection to AI is established through our 
supply-side findings. The digital behaviors captured in our index, including mobile payments, 
online  banking,  digital  transactions,  align  precisely  with  the  platforms  where  financial 
institutions  are  actively  deploying  AI  technologies.  Universal  banks  and  major  e-wallet 
providers reported using AI for fraud detection in mobile transactions, credit scoring for online 
loan applications, chatbots for digital customer service, and transaction monitoring in online 
banking.  As  a  results,  individuals  with  higher  scores  on  our  Digital  Financial  Engagement 
Index are more likely to be interacting with AI-enhanced services, even if they do not recognize 
these features as AI-powered. Thus, digital financial engagement serves as both the behavioral 
foundation for  financial inclusion and the  channel  through which individuals encounter  AI-
enhanced financial services.

5.4 Policy Alignment and Implications

The study also highlights the need for stronger alignment between the National Strategy for 
Financial Inclusion (NSFI 2022–2028) and the National AI Strategy Roadmap 2.0. While the 
NSFI  focuses  on  access  and  affordability,  the  AI  roadmap  emphasizes  innovation  and 
competitiveness. Integrating these frameworks would help ensure that efforts to promote AI-
driven  financial  inclusion  go  hand  in  hand  with  inclusive  AI  governance,  preventing 
technology from widening existing social and economic inequalities.

Overall,  this  study  provides  new  empirical  evidence  that  digital  financial  engagement 
significantly enhances financial inclusion in the Philippines, demonstrating that digital literacy 
and active platform usage have become prerequisites for equitable participation in the financial 
system.  However,  technology  alone  is  not  enough  to  achieve  inclusion.  Persistent 
socioeconomic barriers, institutional disparities, and gaps in digital literacy continue to limit 
participation,  calling  for  stronger  collaboration  among  policymakers,  regulators,  and  the 
private sector. A more inclusive financial landscape in the age of AI should be human-centric, 
data-driven,  and  equity-oriented,  anchored  on  digital  literacy,  strong  infrastructure,  and 
responsible 
improving  data 
governance, and ensuring transparency in AI adoption are critical to building consumer trust 
and  fostering  meaningful  participation  in  the  financial  system.  By  closing  the  gap  between

innovation.  Strengthening  public–private  partnerships,

42

---

<!-- PAGE 49 -->

access  and  trust,  the  Philippines  can  transform  digital  financial  platforms,  enhanced  by  AI 
technologies,  into a true catalyst for inclusive and sustainable growth.

6. Recommendations

Given  these  findings,  several  actionable  measures  are  proposed  to  strengthen  financial 
inclusion through enhanced digital financial platforms and responsible AI deployment in the 
Philippines:

Government  The  evidence  shows  that  digital  financial  platform  engagement  is  a  critical 
determinant of financial inclusion, and that AI technologies can enhance the safety, efficiency, 
and accessibility of these platforms. To maximize its potential, the government must prioritize 
enhancement  of  internet  connectivity  and  electricity  access,  particularly  in  rural  and 
geographically isolated areas. Reliable digital infrastructure is the foundation upon which both 
consumer engagement with digital platforms and institutional AI deployment depend. Ensuring 
digital  networks  are  fast,  reliable,  and  secure  will  enable  more  Filipinos  to  access  digital 
financial services and benefit from AI-driven financial services.

Public-private  partnerships  can  play  a  vital  role  in  expanding  digital  infrastructure  and 
supporting AI-related initiatives. At the same time, laws on data protection, cybersecurity, and 
digital  trust  –  including  the  Data  Privacy  Act  and  Cybercrime  Prevention  Act-  should  be 
regularly reviewed and strengthened to ensure stronger and more effective implementation and 
enforcement.

Since  trust  remains  a  cornerstone  of  digital  adoption,  the  government  should  invest  in 
initiatives  that  enhance  public  confidence  in  AI  and  financial  technologies.  Developing 
national  roadmaps  for  AI  education  and  capacity-building  would  also  be  beneficial,  as 
increased awareness and literacy can empower citizens to use these tools responsibly.

Moreover,  AI-related  indicators,  such  as  AI  literacy,  usage  levels,  and  public  trust,  can  be 
integrated into national surveys to generate reliable data for policymaking. These datasets can 
be used in future studies on the impact of AI on the growth and inclusiveness of the financial 
sector.  The  study  recommends  integrating  AI-related  questions  in  future  rounds  of  BSP’s 
Financial  Inclusion  Surveys  to  measure  directly  consumers’  AI  awareness,  knowledge  and 
usage. Specific questions could include: "Are you aware that the digital financial services you 
use may incorporate AI technologies?"; "Do you trust AI systems to help detect fraud in your 
transactions?"; and "Which specific digital platforms do you use most frequently for financial 
transactions?" Such data would enable future research to differentiate between high-AI and 
low-AI platforms and provide more granular insights into AI's role in financial inclusion.

Education AI adoption in the Philippines remains at an early stage and many consumers use 
AI-powered tools daily, such as chatbots, credit scoring systems, and digital assistants  without 
realizing  it.  Other  individuals  are  not  even  aware  that  these  tools  exist.  This  "invisible  AI" 
paradox means that individuals may benefit from or be affected by AI without understanding 
how  it  works  or  how  to  use  it  effectively.  This  lack  of  awareness  underscores  the  need  for 
education programs that build both financial and AI  literacy, particularly  among vulnerable 
groups (i.e. older population and underserved areas)

The  government,  educational  institutions,  and  private  sector  should  collaborate  to  design 
community-based  training  programs  that  provide  practical  exposure  to  AI  applications  in

43

---

<!-- PAGE 50 -->

financial services. There can also be programs that focus on underserved populations and older 
demographics, who often face greater barriers to digital inclusion.

Furthermore, integrating financial and AI literacy into school curricula and workplace training 
can prepare Filipinos to adapt to the evolving financial landscape. Partnerships with banks and 
fintech companies can help deliver hands-on workshops and public information campaigns that 
clarify AI, promote responsible use, and build consumer trust. Programs that teach individuals 
how  to  identify  fraudulent  schemes,  safeguard  personal  data,  and  understand  algorithmic 
decision-making  can  empower  citizens  to  engage  responsibly  with  AI-driven  financial 
services.

Industry  and  Private  Financial  Institutions  Financial  institutions  in  the  Philippines  have 
begun incorporating AI into their operations, ranging from customer service chatbots, and fraud 
detection systems to predictive credit scoring and personalized recommendations. However, 
our research reveals significant heterogeneity in adoption: while universal banks and major e-
wallet providers have achieved advanced AI deployment, smaller rural banks, cooperatives, 
and savings/loan associations face substantial barriers related to infrastructure, expertise, and 
resources.  In addition,  public scepticism  and  concerns  about data privacy,  algorithmic bias, 
fraud, and cybersecurity continue to hinder widespread adoption.

Financial  institutions  should  prioritize  responsible  AI  deployment  that  addresses  these 
concerns. This includes: (1) ensuring algorithmic transparency and explainability, particularly 
in credit scoring and lending decisions that  affect vulnerable populations; (2) implementing 
robust testing for bias and discrimination in AI models; (3) providing clear communication to 
customers  about  when  and  how  AI  is  being  used  in  their  interactions;  and  (4)  maintaining 
human  oversight  for  high-stakes  decisions.  UnionBank's  Explainable  and  Responsible  AI 
Toolkit, highlighted by our key informant, provides a valuable model for other institutions to 
follow.

To build consumer trust, banks and fintech companies must strengthen security frameworks 
and  enhance  transparency.  Clear  communication  about  data  usage,  coupled  with  strong 
consumer protection protocols, can help build long-term trust in digital financial platforms and 
AI-enhanced services.

In  parallel,  the  government’s  support  in  upgrading  digital  infrastructure  and  enforcing  AI-
related  regulations  is  essential  to  sustain  these  initiatives.  Collaborative  efforts  among 
regulators,  technology  providers,  and  financial  institutions  will  ensure  that  AI  adoption  is 
responsible, inclusive, and aligned with national development goals.

Appendix G. Key Informant Interview Questions

1.  How is the state of Artiﬁcial Intelligence (AI) usage in the ﬁnancial sector? How about in

your current institution?

2.  Based on your experience, what do you see as the most persistent drivers and barriers to

ﬁnancial inclusion in the Philippines today?

3.  How has the growth of digital ﬁnancial services changed access for marginalized groups—

particularly older adults, women, or low-income populations?

4.  Are  ﬁnancial  institutions  equipped  with  the  necessary  infrastructure  and  technology to

facilitate digital ﬁnancial services and capture the beneﬁts of AI?

5.  Given the strong link found between AI exposure and both account ownership and usage, 
what are your institution’s current eﬀorts in promoting digital and ﬁnancial literacy, and 
how might we align these with ﬁnancial inclusion objectives?

6.  Our ﬁndings highlight a persistent digital divide by age. What targeted measures could be

implemented to improve ﬁntech usage among older adults?

7.  Given that AI exposure appears to reduce mistrust in ﬁnancial institutions, what initiatives 
has your institution/agency considered to improve trust and digital conﬁdence among the 
banked and unbanked?

8.  The results suggest that higher income and tertiary education are associated with more 
formal  account  usage,  especially  for  savings  and  credit.  How  can  ﬁnancial  products  be 
restructured to better serve low-income, less-educated groups?

9.  How  do  you  interpret  the  ﬁnding  that  many  users  defer  individual  account  ownership

because someone else in the household already has one?

10. How are you currently addressing barriers related to documentation, trust, and perceived

cost, especially for lower-income clients?

11. How transparent is your institution with its customers/clients regarding the use of AI? 
12. What are the key gaps in digital and AI-related literacy data that hinder better targeting of

ﬁnancial inclusion interventions?

13. How can predictive analytics or AI tools help identify populations at risk of digital exclusion

before they become ﬁnancially excluded?

14. How  do  you  recommend  measuring  “digital  readiness”  for  ﬁnancial  services  at  the

household or community level?

15. What  policy,  product,  or  partnership  would  you  most  like  to  see  implemented  in  the

coming years to accelerate inclusive digital ﬁnance and the use of AI?

Digital Financial Platform Engagement and Financial Inclusion
in the Philippines: Insights on AI Deployment and Policy
Implications
Nikka C. Pesa
Mary Grace R. Agner
Rutcher M. Lacaza
PHILIPPINE INSTITUTE FOR DEVELOPMENT STUDIES
February 2026

Abstract
This paper examines how digital financial platform engagement influences financial inclusion
in the Philippines and explores how financial institutions are deploying artificial intelligence
(AI) in enhancing these platforms. It combines nationally representative data from the World
Bank Global Findex (2021), institutional indicators from the International Monetary Fund
(IMF) Financial Access Survey (2016–2024), and qualitative insights from financial
institutions and policy experts. From the demand-side a Digital Financial Engagement Index
was constructed based on individuals’ usage of mobile payments, online banking, and digital
financial services. Results reveal that digital financial engagement is a strong and consistent
determinant of financial inclusion: individuals who actively use digital financial platforms are
significantly more likely to own and use formal financial accounts. However, several barriers
persist. The lack of money, high perceived costs, documentation issues, and low trust remain
key constraints among Filipino adults, particularly among low-income groups. From the supply
side, institutional data from the IMF Financial Access Survey (2016–2024) and insights from
Key Informant Interviews (KIIs) reveal that financial infrastructure has expanded moderately.
While AI adoption remains nascent and largely concentrated among large, digitally advanced
financial institutions, AI technologies such as fraud detection, credit scoring, and chatbots are
increasingly embedded in the digital platforms where consumer transact. In contrast, smaller
cooperatives and savings and loan associations continue to face structural and resource-related
barriers to adoption. The convergence of demand- and supply-side evidence highlights that
digital financial engagement serves both as a driver and an indicator of financial inclusion,
while AI adoption within these platforms has potential to further enhance access, security, and
user experience. Overall, this study provides new empirical evidence that digital financial
engagement significantly enhances financial inclusion in the Philippines. The study also
documents how financial institutions are strategically deploying AI within these digital
platforms, providing insights for responsible AI adoption that can support inclusive financial
services. By strengthening digital infrastructure, promoting financial and digital literacy, and
ensuring responsible AI adoption, the Philippines can transform digital financial platforms into
a true catalyst for inclusive and sustainable growth.
Keywords: digital financial engagement, artificial intelligence, financial inclusion, digital
platforms, Philippines

iii

Digital Financial Platform Engagement and Financial Inclusion in the
Philippines: Insights on AI Deployment and Policy Implications
Nikka C. Pesa1, Mary Grace R. Agner2, Rutcher M. Lacaza3
1. Introduction
1.1 Background of the Study
Access to formal financial accounts in the Philippines has developed over the years. With the
rise of the internet and the emergence of electronic banking in the 1980’s, Filipinos have
acquired formal financial accounts through automated teller machines (ATMs), internet
banking, and digital wallets (Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas n.d.). A basic measure of financial
inclusion is having possession of an account within a recognized institution, which
encompasses banks, e-money issuers, and microfinance institutions (Bangko Sentral ng
Pilipinas 2021). Years of economic growth have paved the way for several underprivileged
Filipinos to become part of the middle class which led them to experience the benefits of its
increased purchasing power (De Gantès et al. 2023). Also, they note that a young techy-savvy
consumer base is fuelling a heightened demand for innovative financial accounts and services.
According to the recent 2021 Financial Inclusion Survey, formal account ownership among
Filipinos has drastically improved, reaching 56% in 2021 compared to 29% in 2019 with e-
money accounts now being the most owned. However, when comparing it to selected
economies in the Association of Southeast Asian region such as Singapore (97.55%), Thailand
(95.58%), Malaysia (88.37%), and Viet Nam (56.27%), the World Bank (2021) reports that the
Philippines has relatively higher percentage of unbanked and unserved population (51.37%).
Leveraging digital technology can narrow the gap of this segment in accessing and availing
financial services, purchasing products and services, as well as engaging in other transactions.
As the Philippine financial system continues to adapt with emerging technology, traditional
universal and commercial banks are now going heavy in offering online/digital banking for
their consumers following BSP's Digital Payments Transformation Roadmap 2020-2023.
Under this roadmap, one of the BSP’s targets is to have 50% of the total retail transaction
volume be digital. As seen in Figure 1, the BSP reported that 57.4% of the total volume of
retail payments (5,756.5 billion) in 2024 came from digital payments (3,307 billion), with non-
digital payments now holding lesser of the share by 2,449.5 billion (Bangko Sentral ng
Pilipinas n.d.). Targets for digital payments have been increased to 52 to 54% under the
Philippine Development Plan of 2023 to 2028. Figure 1 also shows that these targets have been
met since digital retail payments accounted for 57.4% of total transaction volume according to
the 2024 e-payments measurement conducted by the BSP. Digital payments accounted for
3.307 billion while non-digital payments contributed 2.449 billion to the total transactions
volume.

Moreover, the 2021 Financial Inclusion Survey reported cash as the leading mode of payment,
as seen in Figure 2. While this is the case, with the increasing number of Filipinos with bank
accounts and emergence of digital banks, digital retail payments also increased to 42% in 2022
(Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas, n.d.). The BSP targeted 50% for 2023 given its recent issuance
of licenses to 258 digital payment providers. Because of the increase in digital transactions,
banking fraud and cybercrime has also increased (De Vera 2022). This is the reason why
Filipinos are hesitant to put money in banks (Lim 2024), reluctant to use credit products (Jocson
2023; Yu 2023), and not privy to engaging in more digital transactions (De Gantès et al. 2023).

Artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) are gaining prominence across sectors,
especially in financial services (OECD 2021). Khsetri (2021) highlighted that the fast
advancements in AI and ML are instigating political, economic, and social transformations in
developing economies. According to (Khsetri 2021), AI's revolutionary effects have reshaped
every sector of the economy, including financial services, and it has led to a paradigm shift in
enhancing financial inclusion within an economy. Moreover, adoption of AI and ML in
financial sector offers financial deepening, efficiency, transformation of financial services and
consumers’ interaction with the financial services ecosystem (Khsetri 2021). On the other hand,
2

one should take into account as well that this could likely lead to undesirable effects, such as,
data privacy vulnerabilities, and unfair outcomes for certain consumers like women (Rizzi and
Alvarez 2023), and intensifying the digital divide between advanced and developing economies
(Boukherouaa et al 2021). With these considerations in mind, and building on the discussion
of De Gantès et al. (2023) and the OECD (2021), it is worthwhile to explore the AI deployment
in the Philippine financial sector, including its impact both on those who are already financially
included and on those Filipinos who have yet to be reached by the banking sector and whose
confidence in the financial sector must be increased.
While the potential of AI to advance financial inclusion is clear, studying its direct impact faces
significant methodological challenges. Large-scale household surveys do not yet capture direct
measures of AI awareness, usage, or literacy (World Bank 2021). Moreover, since AI
technologies are often embedded invisibly within digital financial platforms, consumers may
use AI-powered fraud detection or credit scoring without realizing it. To address these
constraints, this study adopts a two-sided approach. On the demand side, the analysis examines
how engagement with digital financial platforms – the primary channels through which AI-
enabled services are delivered – influences financial inclusion. On the supply side, it
investigates how financial institutions deploy AI within these platforms. Collectively, this
framework captures both the behavioral foundations of AI-enabled financial inclusion,
reflected in users’ digital engagement, and the institutional realities of AI adoption.
1.2 Rationale of the Study
The Philippines has made substantial progress in advancing financial inclusion, with formal
account ownership rising significantly in recent years. Despite these gains, a considerable
segment of the population remains either unbanked or underbanked due to barriers such as cost,
distance, lack of documentation, and low trust in financial institutions. At the same time, the
proliferation of digital payments and e-money accounts highlights both the potential and the
complexity of ensuring inclusive and secure participation in the financial system. Artificial
intelligence (AI), increasingly embedded within digital financial platforms, offers new tools to
address these challenges by improving credit assessment, fraud detection, and personalized
financial services. However, its adoption also raises concerns about data privacy, consumer
protection, and the risk of deepening inequalities if safeguards are not in place. This study is
therefore undertaken to examine how digital financial platform engagement influences
financial inclusion and how artificial intelligence can be strategically deployed within these
platforms to enhance accessibility, safety and security, drawing insights from both consumers’
behavior and institutional readiness.
1.3 Statement of the Problem
Recent developments in the Philippine financial sector have indicated that the economy is
undergoing a digital banking revolution (De Gantès et al. 2023) where technology is seen as a
vehicle to boost the financial literacy of Filipinos (Lim 2024). Specifically, the emergence of
AI, including ML, and big data, in the financial sector have opened opportunities for financial
institutions to enhance their presence and reach to Filipino households across all demographic
classifications, posed challenges on the safety and security of banking and making financial
transactions online, and created implications for policymakers to craft frameworks towards
regulation, safety, and security in the use of technology to make transactions. Against this
backdrop, this study poses the following research question: how does engagement with digital
financial platforms influence financial inclusion in the Philippines, and how are financial
3

institutions deploying artificial intelligence within these platforms aiming to enhance
accessibility, safety and security?
1.4 General and Specific Objectives
In addressing the above research question, the study’s general objective is to examine how
digital financial platform engagement influences financial inclusion among Filipino
households, and to inform policymakers how AI can be strategically deployed within these
platforms to enhance access, trust, safety, and security in digital financial transactions. The
following specific objectives are also set.
1. to examine how digital financial platform engagement affects financial inclusion
outcomes from the demand side, and to assess the current state and potential of AI
adoption within financial institutions from the supply side;
2. to provide empirical evidence on the relationship between digital financial engagement
and financial inclusion, and on the opportunities and challenges of AI adoption in the
Philippine financial sector;
3. identify barriers to financial inclusion, including safety, security, trust, and cost
concerns and assess how AI deployment in digital financial platforms can help address
these barriers; and
4. to inform policymakers on strategies for strengthening digital financial infrastructure,
promoting digital and financial literacy, and ensuring responsible AI deployment to
advance inclusive and secure financial services in the Philippines.
1.5 Significance of the Study
Employing advanced digital technology in the financial sector has much opportunity for
advancing the Philippines’s growth (Open Gov Asia 2021). For instance, the Bangko Sentral
ng Pilipinas (2018) highlighted the advantages of digital financial inclusion provides greater
access to formal financial services, economic empowerment and reduced risks and costs
associated with cash. The said institution emphasized its ability to access untapped markets,
facilitate asset accumulation, boost economic participation, decrease cash handling costs, and
mitigate the elevated risks of theft and fraudulent activities linked to the informal economy.
The Philippines is experiencing one of the fastest rates of population growth globally, and the
bankable population is expected to surge from 65 million in 2022 to 85 million by 2030,
marking a 30% growth (De Gantès et al. 2023).
Understanding the relationship between digital financial platform engagement and financial
inclusion, and how artificial intelligence can enhance these platforms, is therefore critical for
maximizing these opportunities. This paper makes several contributions to the growing
literature on digital finance, AI, and financial inclusion in the Philippines. First,
methodologically, the study demonstrates how to research the role of emerging technologies
when direct survey measures are unavailable. By examining digital financial platform
engagement from nationally representative survey data and triangulating this with institutional
evidence of AI deployment through key informant interviews and administrative data, the study
provides a comprehensive picture of how technology is reshaping financial inclusion. This two-
sided approach addresses the challenge of studying AI's effects before survey instruments fully
capture the phenomenon.
4

Second, empirically, the study provides new evidence on the determinants of financial
inclusion in the Philippines, with particular attention to the role of digital financial engagement
and the barriers that persist despite technological advancement. The findings directly inform
the BSP’s efforts to achieve two of its four key outcomes: reducing disparities in financial
inclusion and empowering more consumers to be financially capable (BSP, n.d.).
Third, from a policy perspective, the study offers actionable insights on how AI can be
strategically deployed within digital financial platforms to enhance accessibility, safety, and
security. As pointed out by the OECD (2021), policymakers play a crucial role in fostering
innovation within the financial sector, simultaneously safeguarding the interests of financial
consumers and investors, and maintaining fairness, orderliness, and transparency in the markets
associated with such products and services. This study provides evidence-based guidance for
policymakers on strengthening digital infrastructure, promoting digital and financial literacy,
and ensuring responsible AI adoption, all of which are essential for translating digital financial
engagement into truly inclusive outcomes.
Aside from its relevance to the Philippines, this study, especially its recommended strategies
to policy makers, may also be helpful to other countries, especially those with significant
unbanked and unserved populations. Around 76% of the world’s population have an account
either at a bank or a financial institution, including mobile money service providers. This
percentage may suggest good progress to gauge financial inclusion globally; however,
considering the regional or economy-level view, there exists a wide variation worldwide.
Among the 123 surveyed economies of the Global Financial Inclusion (Global Findex)
Database, “account ownership ranges from just 6% in South Sudan to universal ownership in
high-income economies such as Canada, Germany, and the United Kingdom” (Demirgüç-Kunt
et al. 2021, p. 15). Figure 3 below highlights further this discrepancy. Different economies may
have varied ways of addressing this issue on access to financial services; however, this study
on the Philippines can present useful insights to other countries, especially those facing similar
challenges in leveraging digital platforms and AI to advance financial inclusion.
Figure 3. Adults with an account (%), 2021
Source: Demirgüç-Kunt et al. 2022
5

1.6 Scope and Limitation
Given the nature and objectives of this study, the authors examine how digital financial
platform engagement influences financial inclusion, and how artificial intelligence is being
deployed within these platforms by financial institutions. The study adopts an Explanatory
Sequential Mixed Method Research approach that addresses several important methodological
limitations.
On the demand side the study uses data from the World Bank's Global Financial Inclusion
(Global Findex) Database is utilized. This database comprises nationally representative surveys
conducted among approximately 128,000 adults across 123 economies. The study specifically
uses the 2021 Global Findex Database for the Philippines. However, a key limitation is that the
Global Findex, like most large-scale household surveys, does not include direct questions about
AI awareness, knowledge, literacy, or usage (World Bank 2021). Therefore, the study
constructs a Digital Financial Engagement Index based on individuals' usage of mobile
payments, online banking, and digital financial services. This index measures engagement with
the digital platforms through which AI-enabled services are increasingly delivered, rather than
direct AI exposure or awareness. This limitation means that the demand-side analysis captures
the behavioral foundation for AI-enabled financial inclusion (digital platform usage) rather
than AI interaction itself. Moreover, actual AI exposure varies across platforms and
institutions. An individual using GCash may encounter different AI features than someone
using a small rural bank's digital service, even though both would score similarly on the Digital
Financial Engagement Index. The index cannot distinguish between high-AI and low-AI
platforms based on available survey data. Future research with purpose-designed surveys can
address this limitation by directly measuring AI awareness and platform-specific usage
patterns.
To complement the demand-side analysis and establish the connection to AI, the supply-side
perspective is captured using the IMF’s Financial Access Survey (2016–2024), alongside key
informant interviews with policy experts and private financial institutions were conducted to
capture the supply-side perspective. Findings from both approaches are triangulated to enrich
and contextualize the quantitative results. This triangulation allows the study to demonstrate
how digital financial engagement (measured on the demand side) relates to financial inclusion
outcomes, while documenting the AI deployment patterns (observed on the supply side) within
the digital platforms that users engage with. This two-sided approach addresses the
methodological challenge of studying emerging technologies before comprehensive survey
instruments exist, though it acknowledges the limitations of indirect measurement. The study
does not examine the technical specifications or algorithmic details of specific AI systems (e.g.,
neural network architectures, training datasets, or model performance metrics), as the emphasis
is on behavioral and institutional perspectives rather than computer science or engineering
dimensions of AI deployment.
The study also covers institutional and regulatory aspects related to AI adoption, particularly
issues concerning regulatory capacity, stakeholder engagement, legal frameworks, and
consumer education. These areas are examined to understand how they influence the integrity
and security of financial systems in the context of AI-driven financial inclusion. With these
scope parameters and limitations in mind, the paper aims to guide Philippine policymakers in
crafting evidence-based polices and institutional frameworks that strengthen digital financial
infrastructure, promote digital and financial literacy, and ensure responsible AI deployment to
expand financial access and inclusion.
6

2. Review of Related Literature and Research Gap
This literature review examines existing research on digital financial platforms and financial
inclusion. The review begins with theoretical frameworks for technology adoption (Section
2.1), followed by empirical evidence on how digital financial platforms and AI influence
financial inclusion from both consumer and institutional perspectives (Section 2.2). The review
then examines digital infrastructure and AI readiness in the Philippine context (Section 2.3),
specific applications of artificial intelligence in financial services (Section 2.4); variables
commonly used in financial inclusion studies (Section 2.5), and existing policy frameworks
(Section 2.6). The presentation of the research gap follows.
2.1 Theoretical Frameworks for Technology Adoption
The Technology Acceptance Model (TAM) proposed by Davis (1989) has been instrumental
in explaining users’ behavioral intentions toward adopting new technologies. It posits
individuals who perceive a technology as useful and easy to use are more likely to develop a
positive attitude toward it. In other words, the perceived usefulness and perceived ease of use
are the key determinants influencing attitudes and intentions to adopt a technology. In the
context of digital finance, TAM has been widely used to analyze customers’ intention to adopt
mobile banking, e-wallets, and other financial technologies (Rahman et al. 2023).
However, while TAM provides valuable insights, it tends to underrepresent the roles of trust,
perceived risk, and data security, factors that are increasingly critical in the adoption of
technologies associated with financial data and user privacy (Manser Payne et al. 2018). These
limitations are particularly relevant when studying artificial intelligence (AI) applications in
finance, where concerns about data privacy, algorithmic transparency, and cybersecurity
strongly shape adoption behavior.
To address these gaps, combining TAM with Diffusion of Innovations (DOI) theory has been
widely adopted by researchers (Rogers 1962). Within this context, relative advantage is a key
factor defined as the degree to which a new technology is perceived by users as provding
greater benefits than existing alternatives. By highlighting the added value introduced by the
new technology, relative advantage enhances TAM’s perceived usefulness. In the context of
AI-enabled financial services, this relative advantage may be reflected in improved transaction
security, personalized financial advice, and enhanced fraud detection, all of which strengthen
user confidence and increase the intention to adopt.
2.2 Digital Infrastructure and Institutional Readiness
The Philippines' capacity to expand digital financial services and adopt advanced technologies
depends fundamentally on its digital infrastructure and institutional readiness. Multiple indices
and assessments provide insights into the country's current position and challenges in this
regard. The International Monetary Fund in 2023 constructed an AI Preparedness Index (AIPI)
based on macro-structural indicators. The indicators cover 174 countries' digital infrastructure;
human capital and labor market policies; innovation and economic integration; and regulation
and ethics. As shown in Table 1, the Philippines recorded an overall score of 0.50 which was
slightly higher than Viet Nam (0.48) but relatively lower than Indonesia (0.52), Thailand
(0.54), and Singapore (0.80). Moreover, the Philippines recorded the following values: Digital
Infrastructure (0.10), Innovation and Economic Integration (0.12), Human Capital and Labor
Market Policies (0.15), and Regulation and Ethics (0.12).
7

Broader ASEAN-wide indicators provide additional context on the country's digital and AI
landscape. As presented in Tables 1 and 2, the Philippines occupies a middle position across
multiple dimensions, not only in AI preparedness but also in digital readiness, financial
inclusion indicators, and digital economy size. What distinguishes the country, however, is the
contrast between its very high consumer interest in digital technologies and comparatively
weak institutional capacity. While Filipinos rank among the highest in the region in digital
technology engagement search activity, the ASEAN Digital Integration Index shows that
digital infrastructure and innovation capability remain the country's weakest pillars, which are
precisely the areas needed for financial institutions to design, deploy, and scale technology-
enabled services. This structural "curiosity-capacity gap" helps explain institutional adoption
of advanced technologies such as AI in the financial sector remains gradual despite strong
consumer-driven digital engagement.

On the other hand, the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (2024) reported that the adoption of AI
within the Philippine financial sector is currently limited to specific, narrow applications. The
BSP noted that financial institutions in the country have reached different stages of maturity in
terms of AI adoption and governance. Based on their survey of financial institutions, the most
common AI application is identifying Patterns and Anomalies, which primarily used in Fraud
and Anti-Money Laundering (AML) systems. The report also indicated that most of these
institutions remain conservative in their willingness to take risks associated with AI. There are
existing Philippine studies examining the adoption of AI in terms of customer satisfaction with
mobile banking services (Carandang 2023 and Lim and Parrocho 2024), banking operations
and performance (Jaca et al. 2025), and the perceptions of customers and employees of banking
service and experience (Bragas et al. 2025). Despite these, existing empirical research on how
digital  financial  platform  engagement  influences  financial  inclusion  using  nationally
representative data, and how financial institutions are deploying AI in the Philippines remains
limited. Table 3 shows the summary of the international and local studies on AI in the financial
sector.
